母亲被狗咬伤急救管理知识结构化教学计划的有效性:一项预实验研究

Effectiveness Of a Structured Teaching Programme on Knowledge Regarding First Aid Management of Dog Bite Among Mothers: A Pre-Experimental Study
作者： Ms. Athira Ramachandran, Ms. Aysha Ruksana, Mr. Bajio Sibi, Ms. Clerin Mary, Ms. Drisya E, Ms. Fasilamol P.S, Ms. Fathima Rahmath, Ms. Fathima Sabu, Ms. Fathimath Reema, Ms. Fathimath Yasmin, Ms. Fazna V, Mrs. Roshna KP, Mrs. Shinu Xavier, Roshna Kp, Shinu Xavier
摘要中文翻译背景:狗咬伤构成了一个重大的公共卫生问题,特别是在儿童中,并与身体创伤、伤口感染和致命的 狂犬病 传播 风险相关。母亲作为主要照顾者,在提供即时急救方面发挥着关键作用;然而,他们对狗咬伤的适当急救处理的了解往往不足。结构化教学计划 (STP) 已证明可以有效提高社区环境中的健康相关知识。目的:评估母亲在被狗咬伤后的急救处理方面的考前知识;通过比较测试前和测试后的分数来评估 STP 的有效性;并确定预测试知识与选定的人口统计变量之间的关联。方法:采用实验前一组前测后测设计。通过非概率便利抽样,选择了居住在喀拉拉邦 Malappuram 区 Aliparamba Panchayat Ward-4 的 50 名 (N=50) 9 岁以下儿童母亲。使用自行开发的结构化知识调查问卷收集数据,在数据收集之前确定其内容的有效性和可靠性。提供了包含小册子和演示的 30 分钟 STP。通过专家评审和试点研究确定了可靠性和内容有效性。采用描述性统计、配对t检验和卡方检验进行分析。结果:预测试评估显示,12%的母亲知识较差,78%的母亲知识一般,10%的母亲知识良好(平均8.84,标准差2.38)。测试后的结果显示出明显的进步:76% 的母亲获得了良好的知识,24% 的母亲达到了平均知识,没有人处于贫困类别(平均值 15.72,标准差 1.08)。平均差异为 6.88。配对 t 检验得出 t(49)=19.47 (p < 0.05)。结论:STP 显着提高了母亲对狗咬伤急救处理的了解。建议采取基于社区的教育干预措施,以增强护理人员的能力,减少并发症,并减轻 狂犬病 相关发病率和 死亡率。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本综述重点关注 狂犬病病毒 和 狂犬病 疾病。核心问题是结构化教学计划 (STP) 是否可以提高母亲对狗咬伤急救管理的了解,狗咬伤是与 ZX​​QTERMACQXZ 传播 相关的重大公共卫生问题。
证据基础与方法
该评价是一项预实验研究,采用单组前测后测设计。采用自行开发的结构化知识调查问卷收集数据,并通过专家评审和试点研究验证内容的有效性和可靠性。分析包括描述性统计、配对t检验和卡方检验。
共识与结论
结构化的教学计划显着提高了母亲对狗咬伤急救处理的知识。预测结果显示知识不足,12%的母亲知识较差,而后测结果显示明显改善,没有人处于贫困类别。知识得分的平均差异为6.88,配对t检验具有统计学显着性(t(49)=19.47,p < 0.05)。
争议与缺口
该综述未报告与 狂犬病病毒 或 狂犬病 疾病相关的争议或证据差距。该研究仅限于评估特定人群的知识进步,并未涉及 狂犬病 更广泛的生物学或流行病学方面。
科研与实践启示
建议采取基于社区的教育干预措施,以增强护理人员的能力,减少并发症,并减轻 狂犬病 相关发病率和 死亡率。需要进一步研究来评估此类计划的长期影响及其在不同人群和地区的有效性。

Effectiveness Of a Structured Teaching Programme on Knowledge Regarding First Aid Management of Dog Bite Among Mothers: A Pre-Experimental Study

Authors: Ms. Athira Ramachandran, Ms. Aysha Ruksana, Mr. Bajio Sibi, Ms. Clerin Mary, Ms. Drisya E, Ms. Fasilamol P.S, Ms. Fathima Rahmath, Ms. Fathima Sabu, Ms. Fathimath Reema, Ms. Fathimath Yasmin, Ms. Fazna V, Mrs. Roshna KP, Mrs. Shinu Xavier, Roshna Kp, Shinu Xavier
Original abstract (English)
Background: Dog bites constitute a significant public health problem, particularly among children, and are associated with physical trauma, wound infections, and the risk of fatal rabies transmission. Mothers, as primary caregivers, are pivotal in delivering immediate first aid; however, their knowledge regarding appropriate first aid management of dog bites is often inadequate. Structured teaching programmes (STPs) have demonstrated effectiveness in improving health-related knowledge in community settings. Objectives: To assess pre-test knowledge of mothers regarding first aid management of dog bite; to evaluate the effectiveness of an STP by comparing pre- and post-test scores; and to determine the association between pre-test knowledge and selected demographic variables. Methods: A pre-experimental one-group pre-test post-test design was employed. Fifty (N=50) mothers of children below 9 years residing in Aliparamba Panchayat Ward-4, Malappuram district, Kerala, were selected by non-probability convenience sampling. Data were collected using a self-developed structured knowledge questionnaire, whose content validity and reliability were established before data collection. A 30-minute STP incorporating pamphlets and demonstrations was delivered. Reliability and content validity were established through expert review and a pilot study. Descriptive statistics, paired t-test, and chi-square test were used for analysis. Results: Pre-test assessment revealed that 12% of mothers had poor knowledge, 78% had average knowledge, and 10% had good knowledge (mean 8.84, SD 2.38). Post-test findings showed a marked improvement: 76% of mothers achieved good knowledge and 24% average knowledge, with none remaining in the poor category (mean 15.72, SD 1.08). The mean difference was 6.88. The paired t-test yielded t(49)=19.47 (p 0.05). Conclusion: The STP significantly improved mothers' knowledge regarding first aid management of dog bites. Community-based educational interventions are recommended to empower caregivers, reduce complications, and mitigate rabies-related morbidity and mortality.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on the rabies virus and rabies disease. The central question is whether structured teaching programmes (STPs) can improve mothers' knowledge regarding first aid management of dog bites, which are a significant public health problem associated with rabies transmission.
Evidence base and method
The review is a pre-experimental study employing a one-group pre-test post-test design. Data were collected using a self-developed structured knowledge questionnaire, which was validated for content validity and reliability through expert review and a pilot study. The analysis included descriptive statistics, paired t-test, and chi-square test.
Consensus and conclusions
The structured teaching programme significantly improved mothers' knowledge regarding first aid management of dog bites. Pre-test results indicated inadequate knowledge, with 12% of mothers having poor knowledge, while post-test results showed a marked improvement, with none remaining in the poor category. The mean difference in knowledge scores was 6.88, and the paired t-test was statistically significant (t(49)=19.47, p < 0.05).
Controversies and gaps
The review does not report on controversies or evidence gaps related to the rabies virus or rabies disease. The study is limited to assessing knowledge improvement in a specific population and does not address broader biological or epidemiological aspects of rabies.
Research and practice implications
Community-based educational interventions are recommended to empower caregivers, reduce complications, and mitigate rabies-related morbidity and mortality. Further research is needed to evaluate the long-term impact of such programmes and their effectiveness in different populations and regions.

与帝汶岛GHPR发生相关的因素:文献综述

Faktor-Faktor Yang Berhubungan dengan Kejadian Ghpr di Pulau Timor: Kajian Literatur
作者： Tasalina Yohana Parameswari Gustam, Ronald Pratama Adiwinoto, Stefania Laku Loi
摘要中文翻译狂犬病 是一种导致大脑和神经问题的传染病。这种疾病可以通过已经感染 狂犬病 的动物的唾液或叮咬传播给人类。在印度尼西亚 狂犬病 流行的 26 个省份中,东努沙登加拉省 (NTT) 是受影响最严重的省份。从 2024 年 1 月到 7 月,NTT 记录了 16,180 起可能导致 狂犬病 的动物咬伤事件,并有 27 人因 狂犬病 死亡。本文献综述的目的是研究帝汶岛(印度尼西亚)传播 狂犬病 的动物的危险因素和社会文化行为。本综述使用 2024 年至 2026 年的科学出版物。本次检索时间为2026年4月至9月,检索关键词组合包括狂犬病、GHPR、Timor Island、狂犬病 in Timor Island,主要来源为Google Scholar。随后发现的文章数据确定了影响帝汶岛 狂犬病 控制工作的风险因素以及社会、文化和地理方面。结果表明,风险因素以及社会、文化和地理方面影响 狂犬病 对帝汶岛的控制工作。结论是,帝汶岛发生的GHPR病例是由多种因素造成的,例如养狗数量、对狗的挑衅行为、该地区的狗数量、个人知识、年龄、饲养习惯、与HPR的接触、HPR健康检查和HPR 疫苗接种。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
狂犬病 是一种导致大脑和神经问题的传染病。本文献综述的目的是研究帝汶岛(印度尼西亚)传播 狂犬病 的动物的危险因素和社会文化行为。
证据基础与方法
本综述使用 2024 年至 2026 年的科学出版物。本次检索时间为2026年4月至9月,检索关键词组合包括狂犬病、GHPR、Timor Island、狂犬病 in Timor Island,主要来源为Google Scholar。
共识与结论
结果表明,风险因素以及社会、文化和地理方面影响 狂犬病 对帝汶岛的控制工作。在印度尼西亚 狂犬病 流行的 26 个省份中,东努沙登加拉省 (NTT) 是受影响最严重的省份。
争议与缺口
从 2024 年 1 月到 7 月,NTT 记录了 16,180 起可能导致 狂犬病 的动物咬伤事件,并有 27 人因 狂犬病 死亡。结论是,帝汶岛发生的GHPR病例是由多种因素造成的,例如养狗数量、对狗的挑衅行为、该地区的狗数量、个人知识、年龄、饲养习惯、与HPR的接触、HPR健康检查和HPR 疫苗接种。
科研与实践启示
研究、监测、诊断、治疗、疫苗接种、生态、风险评估或政策的具体优先事项包括了解影响帝汶岛 狂犬病 控制工作的社会文化行为和地理方面。

Faktor-Faktor Yang Berhubungan dengan Kejadian Ghpr di Pulau Timor: Kajian Literatur

Authors: Tasalina Yohana Parameswari Gustam, Ronald Pratama Adiwinoto, Stefania Laku Loi
Original abstract (English)
Rabies is an infectious disease that causes problems in the brain and nerves. This disease can be transmitted to humans through saliva or bites from animals that are already infected with rabies. Among the 26 provinces that are rabies-endemic in Indonesia, East Nusa Tenggara (NTT) is the most affected province. From January to July 2024, NTT recorded 16,180 cases of animal bites that could cause rabies and 27 deaths due to rabies. The purpose of this literature review is to study the risk factors and socio-cultural behaviors of rabies-transmitting animals that occur on Timor Island (Indonesia). This review uses scientific publications from 2024-2026. The search was conducted from April to September 2026. The combination of search keywords includes Rabies, GHPR, Timor Island, rabies in Timor Island, with the main source being Google Scholar. Data from the articles that were found then identified risk factors and social, cultural, and geographical aspects that affect rabies control efforts on Timor Island. The results showed that risk factors and social, cultural, and geographical aspects influence rabies control efforts on Timor Island. The conclusion is that GHPR cases occurring on Timor Island are caused by many factors such as the number of dog ownerships, provocative actions towards dogs, the number of dogs in the area, individual knowledge, age, maintenance practices, contact with HPR, HPR health checks, and HPR vaccination.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Rabies is an infectious disease that causes problems in the brain and nerves. The purpose of this literature review is to study the risk factors and socio-cultural behaviors of rabies-transmitting animals that occur on Timor Island (Indonesia).
Evidence base and method
This review uses scientific publications from 2024-2026. The search was conducted from April to September 2026. The combination of search keywords includes Rabies, GHPR, Timor Island, rabies in Timor Island, with the main source being Google Scholar.
Consensus and conclusions
The results showed that risk factors and social, cultural, and geographical aspects influence rabies control efforts on Timor Island. Among the 26 provinces that are rabies-endemic in Indonesia, East Nusa Tenggara (NTT) is the most affected province.
Controversies and gaps
From January to July 2024, NTT recorded 16,180 cases of animal bites that could cause rabies and 27 deaths due to rabies. The conclusion is that GHPR cases occurring on Timor Island are caused by many factors such as the number of dog ownerships, provocative actions towards dogs, the number of dogs in the area, individual knowledge, age, maintenance practices, contact with HPR, HPR health checks, and HPR vaccination.
Research and practice implications
Specific priorities for research, surveillance, diagnosis, treatment, vaccination, ecology, risk assessment or policy include understanding the socio-cultural behaviors and geographical aspects that affect rabies control efforts on Timor Island.

不断变化的景观中的吸血蝙蝠 狂犬病:土地利用引起的种群连通性变化如何影响 狂犬病病毒 跨物种溢出 的风险

Vampire bat rabies in changing landscapes: how land use-induced changes in population connectivity affect the risk of rabies virus spillover
作者： Germán Botto Nuñez, Richard A. Detomasi Araujo, Lucía Moreira Marrero, Daniel G. Streicker, Raina K. Plowright

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

齐心协力:斯里兰卡朝着人类 狂犬病 零死亡目标迈进 - 世界卫生组织 (WHO)

Stronger together: Sri Lanka advances towards zero human rabies deaths - World Health Organization (WHO)
新闻简报Sri Lanka is making significant progress towards achieving zero human rabies deaths by 2030, as highlighted in a World Health Organization (WHO) news report published on 2026-09-28. The report notes that the country has reduced human rabies deaths to historically low levels through sustained investment, strong government leadership, and coordinated action across human and animal health sectors. In 2025, 14 laboratory-confirmed human rabies deaths were recorded, while nearly 168,000 people received post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) following potential exposure. Over 1.6 million dogs were vaccinated, achieving a national coverage of over 60%. However, 346 animal rabies cases were reported in 2025, indicating that transmission persists in animal populations. The WHO supported an independent external review in September 2025, which identified opportunities to further strengthen surveillance, increase vaccination coverage, and improve data integration. The review emphasized the importance of expanding vaccination among both owned and free-roaming dogs, promoting responsible dog ownership, and enhancing community participation in rabies prevention. These efforts reflect a One Health approach, recognizing the interconnectedness of human, animal, and environmental health. The report underscores the need for continued political commitment, adequate investment, and multisectoral collaboration to sustain elimination efforts and protect public health.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The news report was published on 2026-09-28. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the report discusses progress made in 2025 and ongoing efforts as of September 2025.
地点与对象
该活动以斯里兰卡为中心,特别是世界卫生组织的东南亚区域。受影响的人群包括人类和动物,其中重点是狗介导的 狂犬病 传播。
事件
通过持续投资、强有力的政府领导、人类和动物卫生部门的协调行动以及与世界卫生组织 (WHO) 的合作,斯里兰卡正在努力争取到 2030 年实现人类零死亡。报告证实了这一点,报告强调了该国的进展和正在进行的努力。
规模、影响与风险
In 2025, Sri Lanka recorded 14 laboratory-confirmed human rabies deaths and nearly 168,000 people received post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP). Over 1.6 million dogs were vaccinated, achieving an estimated national coverage of over 60%. Additionally, 346 animal rabies cases were reported in 2025, indicating ongoing transmission in animal populations. The risk is described as requiring continued efforts to interrupt transmission and achieve elimination.
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生部在世卫组织和合作伙伴的技术支持下,于 2025 年 9 月委托进行了一次独立外部审查。审查发现了差距,并建议了支持消除的行动。应对措施正在进行中,努力加强 监测、扩大 疫苗接种 覆盖范围并改善跨部门的数据集成。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Stronger together: Sri Lanka advances towards zero human rabies deaths - World Health Organization (WHO)

News Brief
Sri Lanka is making significant progress towards achieving zero human rabies deaths by 2030, as highlighted in a World Health Organization (WHO) news report published on 2026-09-28. The report notes that the country has reduced human rabies deaths to historically low levels through sustained investment, strong government leadership, and coordinated action across human and animal health sectors. In 2025, 14 laboratory-confirmed human rabies deaths were recorded, while nearly 168,000 people received post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) following potential exposure. Over 1.6 million dogs were vaccinated, achieving a national coverage of over 60%. However, 346 animal rabies cases were reported in 2025, indicating that transmission persists in animal populations. The WHO supported an independent external review in September 2025, which identified opportunities to further strengthen surveillance, increase vaccination coverage, and improve data integration. The review emphasized the importance of expanding vaccination among both owned and free-roaming dogs, promoting responsible dog ownership, and enhancing community participation in rabies prevention. These efforts reflect a One Health approach, recognizing the interconnectedness of human, animal, and environmental health. The report underscores the need for continued political commitment, adequate investment, and multisectoral collaboration to sustain elimination efforts and protect public health.
Original source text (English)

South-East Asia Sri Lanka Global Regions WHO Regional websites Africa Americas South-East Asia Europe Eastern Mediterranean Western Pacific Countries Countries in the WHO South-East Asia Region: Bangladesh Bhutan Democratic People’s Republic of Korea India Maldives Myanmar Nepal Sri Lanka Thailand Timor-Leste Home Health topics News Our work Emergencies About us Multimedia Home Health topics News Our work Emergencies About us Multimedia Home/ News/ Detail/ Stronger together: Sri Lanka advances towards zero human rabies deaths WHO / Sejani Wilegoda Free-roaming dogs at a shelter in Beruwala, Sri Lanka © Credits Stronger together: Sri Lanka advances towards zero human rabies deaths 28 September 2026 Highlights Reading time: 4 min (1154 words) As the world marks World Rabies Day 2026 under the theme “Stronger Together,” Sri Lanka is building on decades of progress and intensifying action to achieve zero human deaths from dog-mediated rabies by 2030. With strong public health services, life-saving post-exposure treatment and sustained dog vaccination, the country has established a firm foundation for elimination. The final stages, however, will require even closer coordination across human health, animal health, local government and communities. Rabies remains one of the world’s most fatal zoonotic diseases, yet it is entirely preventable. Prompt access to post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP), effective surveillance and widespread dog vaccination remain the cornerstones of rabies prevention and control. Globally, dogs are responsible for the overwhelming majority of human rabies infections, making dog vaccination a critical intervention for eliminating the disease. Through decades of sustained investment, strong government leadership and coordinated action across the human health, animal health and local government sectors, Sri Lanka has reduced human rabies deaths to historically low levels and is moving towards its goal of achieving zero human rabies deaths by 2030. This year’s theme reflects a fundamental principle of rabies elimination: success depends on communities, health professionals, veterinarians, policymakers and development partners working together towards a common goal. Decades of progress in rabies control Sri Lanka has a long history of investment in rabies prevention and control. The country established a dedicated Veterinary Public Health Unit within the Ministry of Health in 1953 and launched a comprehensive rabies control programme with support from the World Health Organization (WHO) in 1975. Dog vaccinators administer rabies vaccine to a dog during a field clinic conducted as part of Sri Lanka’s national rabies control programme in Galle, Sri Lanka. Over the years, investments across human and animal health systems have strengthened access to life-saving treatment, expanded laboratory diagnostic capacity, improved surveillance and increased dog vaccination coverage. These efforts have contributed to a substantial decline in human rabies deaths and strengthened the country’s capacity to achieve elimination. Today, the national programme is led by the Public Health Veterinary Services (PHVS) of the Ministry of Health in collaboration with the Epidemiology Unit, Medical Research Institute (MRI), animal health services, local authorities, academic institutions, civil society organizations and development partners. Together, they contribute to a One Health approach that recognizes the close links between human, animal and environmental health and the need for coordinated action to prevent and control rabies. Sustaining progress while addressing remaining challenges According to the Public Health Veterinary Services, Sri Lanka recorded 14 laboratory-confirmed human rabies deaths in 2025. During the same year, nearly 168,000 people received post-exposure prophylaxis following potential exposure to rabies, while more than 1.6 million dogs were vaccinated, achieving an estimated national vaccination coverage exceeding 60%. A trained vaccinator prepares rabies vaccine during a dog vaccination campaign in Galle, Sri Lanka. These achievements reflect the commitment of frontline health workers, public health inspectors, veterinarians, laboratory personnel and community volunteers across the country. However, rabies transmission persists in animal populations. In 2025, 346 animal rabies cases were reported, underscoring the need to sustain and strengthen prevention efforts to interrupt transmission. Strengthening the final push towards elimination To accelerate progress, the Ministry of Health, with technical support from WHO and partners, commissioned an independent external review of Sri Lanka’s rabies programme in September 2025. The review brought together national and international experts from WHO, the World Organisation for Animal Health (WOAH), academia, civil society and government institutions to assess achievements, identify gaps and recommend actions to support elimination. National and international experts participate in the external review of Sri Lanka’s rabies programme in September 2025. The review found that Sri Lanka has built a strong foundation for rabies elimination through sustained programme implementation, access to treatment and vaccination services, and robust public health leadership. It also identified opportunities to further strengthen animal rabies surveillance, increase dog vaccination coverage, improve data integration across sectors and enhance coordination among stakeholders. Experts emphasized the importance of expanding vaccination among both owned and free-roaming dogs, promoting responsible dog ownership, strengthening bite prevention efforts and empowering communities to actively participate in rabies prevention and control. The review also underscored the value of aligning rabies control efforts with broader national initiatives, including the Clean Sri Lanka programme, by supporting environmental cleanliness, sustainable dog population management and safer communities. A field team conducts community awareness activities and vaccination on rabies prevention in Galle, Sri Lanka. Together, these recommendations provide a practical pathway for addressing remaining gaps and accelerating Sri Lanka’s progress towards elimination. WHO support for rabies elimination WHO has been a longstanding partner in Sri Lanka’s rabies control efforts for more than five decades, having supported the development of national strategies and guidelines, strengthened surveillance and laboratory capacity, facilitated technical reviews, promoted a One Health approach and provided technical expertise to support evidence-based decision-making. Most recently, WHO supported the independent external rabies review, helping bring together national and international expertise to assess progress and identify priority actions needed to achieve and sustain zero human rabies deaths. WHO continues to work closely with the Ministry of Health and partners to support implementation of the review recommendations, strengthen surveillance systems, enhance programme monitoring and accelerate progress towards national elimination targets. Towards zero human deaths from rabies The vision of a rabies-free Sri Lanka is increasingly within reach. Achieving and sustaining elimination will require continued political commitment, adequate investment, strong multisectoral collaboration and active participation from communities across the country. Eliminating rabies is more than a public health achievement. It demonstrates how coordinated action across sectors can protect lives, strengthen health systems and improve community well-being. Every vaccinated dog, every appropriately managed animal bite and every informed community brings Sri Lanka one step closer to a future where no one dies from rabies. WHO Representative to Sri Lanka Dr Rajesh Sambhajirao Pandav said: “The theme of this year’s World Rabies Day, ‘Stronger Together,’ reflects Sri Lanka’s experience in advancing rabies prevention and control. The remarkable progress achieved over recent decades has been possible because health professionals, veterinarians, local authorities, policymakers, development partners and communities have worked together towards a shared goal. Sustaining these partnerships will be essential to achieving zero human rabies deaths and creating a rabies-free future for all.” Communities also have a critical role. Vaccinating dogs, supervising owned animals, avoiding unsafe contact with unfamiliar animals, washing bite or scratch wounds immediately with soap and running water, and promptly seeking medical care can prevent rabies deaths. A shared commitment for the future On World Rabies Day, WHO joins the Government of Sri Lanka and partners across the country in reaffirming a shared commitment to ending dog-mediated human rabies. By building on decades of progress and implementing the recommendations of the recent programme review, Sri Lanka can accelerate the final push towards zero human rabies deaths by 2030, protecting future generations from a preventable disease while advancing the shared goal of rabies elimination across the South-East Asia Region. Fact sheets Rabies 17 September 2026 Animal bites 12 January 2024

The risk is described as requiring continued efforts to interrupt transmission and achieve elimination.

The review identified gaps and recommended actions to support elimination. The response is ongoing, with efforts to strengthen surveillance, increase vaccination coverage, and improve data integration across sectors.

2026 Fall Oral Rabies Vaccine Bait Drop to Occur in October in Walker County - Walker County, GA (.gov)
新闻简报乔治亚州沃克县 (.gov) 宣布,美国农业部将于 2026 年 10 月进行口服 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵投放,以预防野生动物感染 狂犬病 并保护人类和宠物。正如官方通知所述,这是已确认的事件。该举措是降低 狂犬病 传播 风险的更广泛公共卫生战略的一部分。证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。该活动定于 10 月举行,目前没有关于投放诱饵的确切日期或地点的更多细节。响应状态表明 疫苗接种 工作已计划并确认,没有提及未解决的信息或来源限制。该县的网站是此信息的官方来源。

时间

地点与对象
佐治亚州沃克县;受影响人群:人和宠物。
事件
农业部将于 10 月在沃克县投放口服 狂犬病 狂犬病 (ORV) 诱饵,以防止野生动物感染 狂犬病,以保护人和宠物。这是佐治亚州沃克县 (.gov) 网站报道的已确认事件。
规模、影响与风险
ORV诱饵滴剂旨在预防野生动物中的狂犬病,这可以间接降低狂犬病 传播对人和宠物的风险。所提供的证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
该活动已确认并计划于 2026 年 10 月举行。响应措施包括美国农业部的 疫苗接种 努力。没有提及未解决的信息或来源限制。

2026 Fall Oral Rabies Vaccine Bait Drop to Occur in October in Walker County - Walker County, GA (.gov)

News Brief
Walker County, GA (.gov) announced that the U.S. Department of Agriculture will conduct an oral rabies vaccine bait drop in October 2026 to prevent rabies in wildlife and protect people and pets. This is a confirmed event, as stated in the official notice. The initiative is part of a broader public health strategy to reduce the risk of rabies transmission. No specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported in the evidence. The event is scheduled for October, with no further details on the exact dates or locations of the bait drop. The response status indicates that the vaccination effort is planned and confirmed, with no unresolved information or source limitations mentioned. The county's website serves as the official source for this information.

狂犬病:从巴斯德 疫苗 到一种健康 - tribuneindia.com

RABIES: FROM PASTEURS VACCINE TO ONE HEALTH - tribuneindia.com
新闻简报狂犬病 是一种由狂犬病毒引起的致命人畜共患疾病,几个世纪以来一直令人恐惧。19世纪之前,一旦出现症状,就没有有效的干预措施。1885年,路易斯·巴斯德(Louis Pasteur)利用来自兔子脊髓组织的弱化狂犬病病毒开发了一种暴露后治疗,这拯救了被狂犬病狗咬伤的约瑟夫·梅斯特(Joseph Meister)。这一突破标志着狂犬病预防的开始。尽管取得了进步,狂犬病 每年仍然在全球造成约 59,000 人死亡,其中大部分是资源匮乏地区的儿童。通过提示暴露后预防和群狗疫苗接种,这种疾病是完全可以预防的。2007 年,世界卫生组织 (WHO) 和全球 狂犬病 控制联盟开始将 9 月 28 日定为世界 狂犬病 日。WHO、FAO 和 WOAH 制定了 2030 年目标,以结束由狗介导的 狂犬病 造成的人类死亡。“同一个健康”方法认识到人类、动物和环境健康之间的相互联系,是对抗 狂犬病 的核心。它要求印度公共卫生和兽医系统之间进行机构协调,以有效管理该疾病。
查看新闻五要素
时间
几个世纪以来,狂犬病 一直让人们感到恐惧,路易斯·巴斯德 (Louis Pasteur) 的第一个人类 狂犬病 发生于 1885 年,世界 狂犬病 日始于 2007 年。世界卫生组织和全球 狂犬病 控制联盟自 2007 年起就开始纪念这一日期。
地点与对象
狂犬病 是由狂犬病病毒引起的,主要通过受感染的哺乳动物的唾液传播,其中绝大多数是印度以及亚洲和非洲大部分地区的狗。
事件
Louis Pasteur用含有弱化狂犬病病毒的兔子脊髓组织开发了暴露后治疗,并于1885年给Joseph Meister注射。狂犬病是一种人畜共患疾病,其预防依赖于暴露后预防和大众狗疫苗接种。
规模、影响与风险
狂犬病 每年仍然在全球造成约 59,000 人死亡,其中大部分是生活在资源匮乏地区的儿童。通过及时的暴露后预防和群狗疫苗接种是完全可以预防的。
应对、状态与不确定性
这种疾病是可以预防的,WHO、FAO 和 WOAH 已制定了 2030 年目标,以结束由狗介导的 狂犬病 造成的人类死亡。“同一个健康”方法强调公共卫生和兽医系统之间的协调。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

RABIES: FROM PASTEURS VACCINE TO ONE HEALTH - tribuneindia.com

News Brief
Rabies, a deadly zoonotic disease caused by a lyssavirus, has been a source of fear for centuries. Before the 19th century, there was no effective intervention once symptoms appeared. In 1885, Louis Pasteur developed a post-exposure treatment using weakened rabies virus from rabbit spinal cord tissue, which saved Joseph Meister after he was bitten by a rabid dog. This breakthrough marked the beginning of rabies prevention. Despite advances, rabies still kills approximately 59,000 people annually worldwide, mostly children in low-resource regions. The disease is entirely preventable through prompt post-exposure prophylaxis and mass dog vaccination. In 2007, the World Health Organization (WHO) and the Global Alliance for Rabies Control began marking September 28 as World Rabies Day. The WHO, FAO, and WOAH have set a 2030 target to end human deaths from dog-mediated rabies. The One Health approach, which recognizes the interconnectedness of human, animal, and environmental health, is central to combating rabies. It demands institutional coordination between public health and veterinary systems in India to manage the disease effectively.
Original source text (English)
History Rabies had terrified people for centuries before anyone understood it. Ancient physicians recognised the link between a dog's bite and a fatal, agonising illness in humans, but had no way to intervene once symptoms began. That gap between exposure and certain death is what made the disease so feared, and it is what a 19th-century French chemist set out to close. What it is Louis Pasteur, working with rabbit spinal-cord tissue containing weakened rabies virus, developed a post-exposure treatment. On 6 July 1885 he administered it to nine-year-old Joseph Meister, bitten repeatedly by a rabid dog; the boy survived. Pasteur died on 28 September 1895, and since 2007 the date has been marked as World Rabies Day by the WHO and the Global Alliance for Rabies Control. Rabies is caused by a lyssavirus transmitted mainly through the saliva of infected mammals, overwhelmingly dogs in India and much of Asia and Africa. Once clinical symptoms appear, it is almost universally fatal, but it is entirely preventable: post-exposure prophylaxis works if given promptly, and mass dog vaccination interrupts transmission at its source. Timeline: - 1885 Pasteur's first human treatment - 1985 modern cell-culture vaccines replace nerve-tissue vaccines - 2007 World Rabies Day begins - 2015 WHO, FAO and WOAH set a 2030 target to end human deaths from dog-mediated rabies. Significance Rabies still kills roughly 59,000 people a year worldwide, disproportionately children in low-resource settings, almost entirely preventable by vaccinating dogs and treating bite victims in time. It has become the textbook case for One Health, the recognition that human, animal and environmental health cannot be managed in isolation. Key points of relevance from UPSC angle - Zoonotic disease → animal reservoir → human spillover → vaccination as control strategy - One Health → convergence of GS-III science and technology with public health and environment - India's National Rabies Control Programme and dog population management Expected questions - (Prelims) Rabies is caused by a pathogen belonging to which family: (a) Rhabdoviridae (b) Poxviridae (c) Flaviviridae (d) Coronaviridae? - (Mains) Examine the One Health approach with reference to a zoonotic disease of your choice. What institutional coordination does it demand between public health and veterinary systems in India? Key Words Zoonosis | Louis Pasteur | Post-exposure prophylaxis | One Health | Dog-mediated rabies | Vaccine-preventable disease | Animal reservoir
View five news elements
Time
Rabies has terrified people for centuries, with Louis Pasteur's first human treatment occurring in 1885 and World Rabies Day beginning in 2007. The WHO and Global Alliance for Rabies Control mark the date since 2007.
Location and population
Rabies is caused by a lyssavirus transmitted mainly through the saliva of infected mammals, overwhelmingly dogs in India and much of Asia and Africa.
Event
Louis Pasteur developed a post-exposure treatment using rabbit spinal-cord tissue containing weakened rabies virus, which was administered to Joseph Meister in 1885. Rabies is a zoonotic disease, and its prevention relies on post-exposure prophylaxis and mass dog vaccination.
Scale, impact and risk
Rabies still kills roughly 59,000 people a year worldwide, disproportionately children in low-resource settings. It is entirely preventable through timely post-exposure prophylaxis and mass dog vaccination.
Response, status and uncertainty
The disease is preventable, and the WHO, FAO, and WOAH have set a 2030 target to end human deaths from dog-mediated rabies. The One Health approach emphasizes coordination between public health and veterinary systems.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

伊朗与世卫组织共同努力到2030年消灭人类狂犬病

Iran, WHO join efforts to eliminate human rabies by 2030
新闻简报2026 年 9 月 29 日,伊朗和世界卫生组织 (WHO) 联手通过在世界 狂犬病 日研讨会上会签的国家计划,到 2030 年消灭人类 狂犬病。该倡议强调“同一个健康”方法,要求人类健康、兽医服务、市政当局和社区之间进行合作。过去二十年来,报告的动物咬伤案件增加了五倍,在过去的伊朗年(2025 年 3 月至 2026 年 3 月)有 20 人死亡。狂犬病 是完全可以预防的,但如果不及时治疗,仍然是致命的。世卫组织驻伊朗代表Jean Jabbour博士指出,99%的人类狂犬病病例是由狗咬伤引起的,其中流浪狗占这些病例的一半以上。该计划包括全国729个狂犬病预防和治疗中心,伊朗巴斯德研究所支持诊断和培训。2026 年世界 狂犬病 日的主题是“共同更强”,强调了互连系统和合作伙伴关系的必要性,以实现消除 狂犬病。
查看新闻五要素
时间
《2026-2030 年消除人类 狂犬病 国家计划》在 2026 年 9 月 29 日纪念世界 狂犬病 日的研讨会上会签。2026 年 9 月 28 日的主题是“共同更强”。在过去的二十年里,报告的动物咬伤案件增加了五倍。
地点与对象
该倡议主要针对伊朗,在过去的伊朗年(2025 年 3 月至 2026 年 3 月),伊朗有超过 441,000 人被动物咬伤。狂犬病 是该国的一个公共卫生问题,同期有 20 人因动物咬伤而丧生。
事件
伊朗和世界卫生组织 (WHO) 共同努力,通过在世界 狂犬病 日研讨会上会签的国家计划,到 2030 年消灭人类 狂犬病。该活动强调了“同一个健康”方法、跨部门合作的重要性,以及动物咬伤后立即就医的必要性。
规模、影响与风险
狂犬病 是完全可以预防的,但如果不及时解决,仍然是致命的。在伊朗,每 5 例确诊的 狂犬病 死亡中,约有 4 例是由狗咬伤造成的,其中一半以上是流浪狗咬伤。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织代表 Jean Jabbour 博士强调了多部门合作的必要性,包括狗的 疫苗接种、流浪狗的管理和公众意识。国家计划包括在所有省份运营的 729 个 狂犬病 预防和 治疗 中心,伊朗巴斯德研究所为医务人员提供诊断支持和培训。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Iran, WHO join efforts to eliminate human rabies by 2030

News Brief
On September 29, 2026, Iran and the World Health Organization (WHO) joined forces to eliminate human rabies by 2030 through a national plan countersigned at a World Rabies Day seminar. The initiative emphasizes a One Health approach, requiring collaboration among human health, veterinary services, municipalities, and communities. Over the past two decades, there has been a fivefold increase in reported animal bite cases, with 20 deaths in the past Iranian year (March 2025–March 2026). Rabies is entirely preventable but remains fatal if not treated promptly. Dr Jean Jabbour, WHO Representative in Iran, noted that 99% of human rabies cases are due to dog bites, with stray dogs accounting for over half of these cases. The plan includes 729 rabies prevention and treatment centers nationwide, and the Pasteur Institute of Iran supports diagnosis and training. The 2026 World Rabies Day theme, ‘Stronger Together,’ underscores the necessity of interconnected systems and partnerships to achieve rabies elimination.
Original source text (English)
About us Membership Contact us Sun October 4, 2026 Home Society Economy Politics Sports Culture International Multimedia Tourism Iran, WHO join efforts to eliminate human rabies by 2030 Society September 29, 2026 - 15:37 TEHRAN – The National Plan for the Elimination of Human Rabies 2026–2030 was formally countersigned at a seminar marking World Rabies Day, hosted by the Ministry of Health and Medical Education's Centre for Communicable Disease Control. The initiative aims to strengthen national efforts to prevent and ultimately eliminate human rabies through coordinated measures in disease surveillance, prevention, public awareness, and timely access to post-exposure treatment. Speaking at the event, Dr Jean Jabbour, WHO Representative in the Islamic Republic of Iran, emphasized the need for collaboration across human health, veterinary services, municipalities, communities and other sectors through a One Health approach. He also highlighted timely care after animal bites and dog vaccination as essential measures to prevent rabies deaths Achieving the goal of zero human deaths from rabies by 2030 requires enhancing collaborations among the health system, veterinary, municipalities, and local communities within the framework of the “One Health” approach, Jabbour stated. Dog bites contribute to 99 percent of human rabies cases. Despite being fatal, rabies is entirely preventable. However, no single organization can defeat the disease on its own, the health ministry’s website quoted Jabbour as saying. The official made the remarks on the occasion of World Rabies Day, which is marked on September 28 every year. Highlighting this year’s World Rabies Day theme, ‘Stronger Together’, he noted that rabies elimination needs ongoing multi-sectoral cooperation among physicians, veterinarians, health staff, municipalities, and local communities. Referring to the number of animal bites in the country, he said over the past two decades there has been a fivefold increase in the number of reported cases. More than 441,000 individuals bitten by animals were referred to medical centers in the past Iranian year (March 2025 – March 2026). Twenty of them lost their lives, and it could have been prevented. Eleven individuals died without receiving any treatment. The statistics indicate that, in addition to prompt presentation of the bitten person to medical centers, timely and appropriate post-exposure care and the raise of public awareness are critical, and every case of animal bite must be treated as a medical emergency. When a person is bitten or scratched by an animal, even if the bite looks small, they have to visit a healthcare provider right away, he added. Noting that approximately four out of every five confirmed rabies deaths in Iran are due to dog bites, Jabbour stated that stray dogs alone account for more than half of these cases, while the rest are attributed to wild animals, including wolves and foxes. The official underlined the role of One Health strategies for rabies control, including constant vaccination of dogs, effective management of stray dogs, and the promotion of close cooperation among the human health, environmental health, and veterinary sectors, he added. Lauding the adopted measures in the country, Jabbour said some 729 rabies prevention and treatment centers are operating across the country, and post-exposure care services are provided in all provinces. Around one million doses of human rabies vaccine and 180,000 vials of anti-rabies serum are procured for the national program, annually; Pasteur Institute of Iran helps diagnose confirmed cases of rabies as well as train physicians and health workers. The official went on to say that the development of a national strategic document for rabies eradication is also a significant step toward aligning Iran with the global goal of “zero human deaths from dog-mediated rabies by 2030”, adding that the WHO is committed to supporting Iran, through technical assistance and collaboration, in achieving the goal. Why “Stronger Together”? The 2026 theme, ‘Stronger Together’, highlights a simple truth: rabies elimination is only possible when we work together. Expand access to life-saving dog vaccinations. Improve rabies surveillance and reporting systems. Strengthen public awareness and education. Support policies that protect both people and animals. Build sustainable community-led solutions. Rabies elimination depends on connected systems. Stronger partnerships create stronger outcomes! We all have a role to play From local communities and veterinary professionals to governments, healthcare workers, educators, and global partners, we all have role to play in making a world free from rabies a reality. Communities: Communities are often the first line of defense against rabies. Education, awareness, and responsible animal care help prevent cases before they happen. Veterinary Professionals: Mass dog vaccination remains the most effective way to stop rabies at its source. Healthcare Workers: Timely treatment and accurate diagnosis save lives. Governments and Policymakers: Strong legislation, investment, and national coordination are essential for long-term progress. Global Partners: Collaboration across sectors helps share resources, expertise, and solutions that accelerate impact. One Health in action Rabies elimination depends on interconnected action. Education, vaccination, surveillance, and legislation cannot work in isolation, they must function as part of one coordinated system. World Rabies Day 2026 reinforces the importance of a One Health approach, recognizing the connection between human, animal, and environmental health. Time
The National Plan for the Elimination of Human Rabies 2026–2030 was countersigned at a seminar marking World Rabies Day on September 29, 2026. The theme for 2026 was ‘Stronger Together’ on September 28, 2026. Over the past two decades, there has been a fivefold increase in reported animal bite cases.
Location and population
The initiative is focused on Iran, where more than 441,000 individuals were bitten by animals in the past Iranian year (March 2025 – March 2026). Rabies is a public health concern in the country, with 20 individuals losing their lives from animal bites in the same period.
Event
Iran and the World Health Organization (WHO) joined efforts to eliminate human rabies by 2030 through a national plan countersigned at a World Rabies Day seminar. The event emphasized the importance of a One Health approach, collaboration across sectors, and the need for prompt medical attention after animal bites.
Scale, impact and risk
Rabies is entirely preventable but remains fatal if not addressed promptly. Approximately 4 out of every 5 confirmed rabies deaths in Iran are due to dog bites, with stray dogs accounting for more than half of these cases.
Response, status and uncertainty
The WHO representative, Dr Jean Jabbour, highlighted the need for multi-sectoral cooperation, including vaccination of dogs, management of stray dogs, and public awareness. The national plan includes 729 rabies prevention and treatment centers operating across all provinces, and the Pasteur Institute of Iran provides diagnostic support and training for medical personnel.

Rabies: A Deadly Zoonotic Disease and Prevention Efforts - Ratopati

News Brief
The article discusses rabies, a deadly zoonotic disease caused by the rabies virus (Lyssavirus), and highlights Nepal's efforts to control it. Rabies primarily affects the nervous system and is 100% fatal once symptoms appear, but it is 100% preventable with immediate wound cleaning and vaccination. Nepal has committed to achieving zero human deaths from dog-bite transmitted rabies by 2030, implementing a One Health Policy, mass dog vaccination campaigns, and public awareness programs. Bhutan's success in eradicating dog-transmitted rabies since 2023 is cited as a model for Nepal. The World Health Organization has recognized Bhutan's achievement as the first country in Southeast Asia to do so. The article emphasizes the importance of systematic management of stray dogs, free treatment for bite victims, and collaboration between health and animal health sectors. It also notes the societal and economic impacts of rabies, including fear, anxiety, and financial burdens. The article was published on September 28, 2026, by Ratopati, and it is a full report with no source limitations.
Original source text (English)
Main News News Health & Lifestyle Business Sports World Entertainment Technology English नेपाली हिन्दी Main News News Health & Lifestyle Business Sports World Entertainment Technology Rabies: A Deadly Zoonotic Disease and Prevention Efforts Ratopati September 28, 2026 Read in Nepali Shares The coexistence of human civilization and the animal kingdom is old, but amidst this coexistence, some deadly diseases have added serious challenges to public health. Among them, the most terrifying and 100% fatal disease is rabies. What is Rabies? Caused by the rabies virus (Lyssavirus), this is a type of 'zoonotic' (transmissible from animals to humans) disease. This virus primarily affects the nervous system of humans and other mammals. After infection, as the virus spreads within the body and reaches the brain, severe symptoms such as fear of water and air (hydrophobia and aerophobia), restlessness, drooling, biting tendencies, and paralysis appear in the patient. Once the symptoms become clear, the patient's death is almost certain. Mode of Transmission The rabies virus is present in the saliva of infected animals. This disease is mainly transmitted through the following means- Animal Bites: When a rabid dog, fox, wolf, monkey, or other mammal bites or scratches. Saliva Entering Broken Skin or Wounds: If an infected animal licks an existing wound or cut on the body. Efforts Made in Nepal to Control Rabies Nepal has committed to the global goal of achieving zero human deaths from dog-bite transmitted rabies by 2030. For this, the following efforts are being made at government and non-governmental levels. One Health Policy: A strategy is being adopted to control diseases by integrating human health, animal health, and environmental sectors. Stray Dog Management: Mass dog vaccination campaigns and sterilization programs are being conducted. Public Awareness Enhancement: Awareness is being spread at school and community levels about washing bite wounds with soap and water for 15 minutes and getting vaccinated immediately. Successful Examples of Rabies Control Worldwide Inspiration from Bhutan Our neighboring country Bhutan has recently (in 2026) achieved a historic success recognized by the World Health Organization by completely eradicating rabies transmitted by dogs as a public health problem. Bhutan achieved this success by vaccinating more than 90 percent of its stray and pet dogs against rabies continuously, controlling the dog population, and providing immediate post-exposure vaccination to humans even in remote locations. Bhutan has succeeded in sterilizing 100 percent of free-roaming dogs. Dogs have been identified and registered. The old practice of culling dogs has been moved towards systematic management. Treatment after being bitten by a dog has been made free and accessible. Health and animal health sectors have been linked in a single campaign. Continuous monitoring of such stray dogs and cooperation at the border areas are also being carried out. The main thing is that the World Health Organization has stated that human deaths due to rabies transmitted by dogs have been zero in Bhutan since 2023. Based on this, in 2026, the World Health Organization recognized Bhutan as the first country in the South-East Asian region to achieve such a feat. Bhutan's model can also be a great guide for Nepal. Societal Impacts of Rabies Rabies is not just a health problem; it also has serious impacts on social and economic structures. Economic Burden: After infection, the cost of medicines, vaccines, and hospitals increases for both individuals and the state. Environment of Fear and Anxiety: Due to the fear of stray dog bites and rabies in the community, insecurity is created in the movement of children, the elderly, and pedestrians. Livestock Loss: Farmers suffer significant economic losses due to the death of cattle and other domestic animals because of rabies. Superstition and Lack of Awareness: In villages, the wrong tradition of going to traditional healers or applying herbs after being bitten still leads to people losing their lives prematurely. Although rabies is 100% fatal, it is 100% preventable. If the wound is cleaned with soap and water for 15 minutes immediately after being bitten by an animal and timely anti-rabies vaccination is received at a nearby health facility, one can be completely protected from rabies. Learning from the success achieved by Bhutan, if Nepal can enforce strict policies, manage dog vaccinations, and take awareness to the grassroots level, it is not impossible to make Nepal rabies-free by 2030. (-The author is a Veterinary Doctor.) Disclaimer This specific news has been automatically translated by AI. 随着 狂犬病 在 LI 浣熊中传播,工人们用 疫苗 诱饵做出反应 - 新闻日报

As rabies spreads in LI raccoons, workers respond with vaccine bait - Newsday
新闻简报狂犬病 正在长岛的浣熊中传播,特别是在萨福克县和拿骚县,促使卫生官员实施 疫苗 诱饵计划。自 2025 年 2 月以来,萨福克郡已记录了 51 起陆地 狂犬病 病例,涉及 49 只浣熊和 2 只猫,而拿骚在 2025 年出现了 27 起病例,涉及 26 只浣熊和 1 只流浪猫。疫苗 诱饵以鱼味调味,分布在林区和其他浣熊栖息地。官员们指出,该计划已显示出有效性,26% 的浣熊抗体检测呈阳性,超过了典型的 10%-15% 的反应率。建议居民避免接触野生动物并确保他们的宠物接种疫苗。纽约州最后一次确诊的人类 狂犬病 病例是在 30 多年前,预防措施包括 监测、分发 疫苗 以及为宠物免费注射 狂犬病。暴发 于 2024 年在拿骚开始,现已蔓延到萨福克,巴比伦、湾岸和国王公园等地区都有病例报告。州卫生部门强调,狂犬病 通过唾液传播,通常是通过咬伤或接触开放性伤口传播,而宠物是防止人类接触病毒的关键屏障。该计划旨在提供群体免疫力并降低人们感染 狂犬病 传播 的风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 于 2024 年在拿骚县开始,并在去年传播至 Suffolk 县。最新的诱饵投放计划于 2026 年 10 月宣布,Suffolk 县于上个月分发了 疫苗 诱饵。发表日期为 2026-10-02。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在长岛,特别是萨福克县和拿骚县。受影响的人群包括浣熊和流浪猫,自 2025 年 2 月以来,萨福克县记录了 51 例病例,2025 年拿骚县记录了 27 例病例。
事件
狂犬病 正在长岛的浣熊中传播,卫生官员正在分发口服 狂犬病 疫苗 诱饵来控制 狂犬病。萨福克县卫生服务部和拿骚县卫生局已实施这些措施。
规模、影响与风险
自 2025 年 2 月以来,萨福克县已记录了 51 例陆生 狂犬病 病例,涉及 49 只浣熊和 2 只猫。在拿骚县,2025年病例数跃升至27例,其中有26只浣熊和1只流浪猫。疫苗 诱饵计划已显示出一定的有效性,26% 的浣熊表现出抗体反应。建议居民避免接触野生动物并确保他们的宠物接种疫苗。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在积极分发 疫苗 诱饵并开展 监测 计划。他们还为宠物提供免费的 狂犬病 疫苗接种 诊所。该计划的有效性正在接受监测,纽约州最后一次确诊的人类 狂犬病 病例已经是 30 多年前了。来源是完整的原始报告,信息基于确诊病例和官方声明。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As rabies spreads in LI raccoons, workers respond with vaccine bait - Newsday

News Brief
Rabies is spreading among raccoons in Long Island, particularly in Suffolk and Nassau Counties, prompting health officials to implement a vaccine baiting program. Since February 2025, 51 terrestrial rabies cases have been documented in Suffolk, involving 49 raccoons and 2 cats, while Nassau saw 27 cases in 2025, with 26 raccoons and 1 stray cat. The vaccine baits, flavored with fish, are distributed in wooded areas and other raccoon habitats. Officials note that the program has shown effectiveness, with 26% of raccoons testing positive for antibodies, surpassing the typical 10%-15% response rate. Residents are advised to avoid contact with wildlife and ensure their pets are vaccinated. The last confirmed human rabies case in New York State was over 30 years ago, and preventive measures include surveillance, vaccine distribution, and free rabies shots for pets. The outbreak, which began in Nassau in 2024, has spread into Suffolk, with cases reported in areas like Babylon, Bay Shore, and Kings Park. The state Health Department emphasizes that rabies is transmitted through saliva, often via bites or contact with open wounds, and that pets serve as a critical barrier against human exposure. The program aims to provide herd immunity and reduce the risk of rabies transmission to people.

Learn more A raccoon at an undisclosed Suffolk County location feeds on oral rabies vaccine bait. Credit: Suffolk County Department of Health Services By Lisa L. Colangelolisa.colangelo@newsday.com@lisalcolangeloUpdated October 3, 2026 5:22 pm Share Raccoons will eat just about anything, especially if it's covered with cod liver oil and fish meal crumbles. That’s what health officials on Long Island are hoping, as workers distribute hundreds of thousands of fish-flavored packets containing rabies vaccine in wooded areas, culverts and other places raccoons like to gather. Vaccine baiting for raccoons is an important weapon against a wildlife rabies outbreak that started in Nassau County in 2024 and spread into Suffolk County last year. Since February 2025, 51 cases of terrestrial rabies have been documented in Suffolk County — in 49 raccoons and two cats. Before that, there had not been a case since 2009, officials said. Terrestrial rabies cases do not include bats, which also carry the virus. WHAT NEWSDAY FOUND The local outbreak of terrestrial wildlife rabies on Long Island, which includes raccoons and some cats, has been moving farther into Suffolk County in recent months. Health officials are placing rabies vaccine baits, flavored with fish, to attract raccoons. Once they bite the bait, the vaccine will enter their system and provide protection from the virus. Residents are warned to stay away from wildlife and make sure their pets have updated rabies vaccines. Pets are an important barrier between the virus in wildlife and people. Oral rabies vaccine baits, covered in cod liver oil and other substances, that were distributed by the Nassau County Department of Health. Credit: Credit: Nassau County Department of Health Most of the cases were clustered in Amityville, with a few in Lindenhurst, Deer Park and Wyandanch. But over the past few months, they also have been found farther east and north in Babylon, Bay Shore, Islip, Northport, Smithtown and Kings Park. Sign up for the Health newsletter Get the latest stories every week about health and wellness, covering topics from medicine and mental health to updates on the coronavirus and new research. Sign up By clicking Sign up, you agree to our privacy policy. A raccoon picked up in Manorville in June after an encounter with a local resident tested positive for rabies. Health officials believe it likely was relocated from another area since its location was far from where other raccoons with rabies were found. Preventive measures With surveillance, vaccine baits and free rabies shots for pets, Suffolk County is trying to hold the line. Rabies is a potentially deadly virus that is transmitted via the saliva of an infected animal. That can happen through a bite or by the infected saliva getting into an open wound, eyes, nose or mouth, according to the state Health Department. While fatal human cases of rabies are rare — the last one in New York State was more than 30 years ago and involved an encounter with a bat — they also can be avoided with prompt medical treatment. Suffolk County already has distributed about 240,000 vaccine bait packets, and Nassau will put out more than 144,000 by Oct. 9. “The goal of the bait program is to provide herd immunity,” said Dr. Meghan Davis, a veterinarian and chair of the master of public health program at Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. “But you are not going to catch every member of the herd. ... It’s about serving as a line of defense.” The vaccine baits are making a difference, Suffolk County Health Commissioner Dr. Gregson Pigott told Newsday. He said U.S. Department of Agriculture staff trapped and tested blood from numerous raccoons in the areas that were baited last year to determine the effectiveness of the program. The tests showed 26% of raccoons showed an antibody response, above the average of 10%-15% that the USDA typically finds following a first-time baiting program. Staying away from wildlife is the best way to avoid any kind of illness, state officials said, advising people to contact a rehabilitator if they believe an animal is sick or injured. If a person or pet is in contact with or bitten by a wild animal, they should seek immediate medical treatment and contact their county's health department. Companion dogs, cats and ferrets must be vaccinated against rabies under New York State law, the first shot no later than four months after birth and the second within a year later. Booster shots are required at different intervals, one year or three years, depending on the animal and vaccine. Vaccinating pets Davis pointed out that while the baiting program is designed to reduce how many wild animals become rabid and infectious, dog and cat vaccines protect people by providing a shield for the animals most likely to infect them. “Especially if you live on Long Island,” said Dr. Carly Fox, senior veterinarian for urgent and emergency care at the Schwarzman Animal Medical Center in Manhattan. “If you let your dog outside or if you have a cat that goes outside, they can experience interactions with wildlife that you just don't witness.” In two separate Kings Park incidents, dogs and their owners came in contact with raccoons that later tested positive for rabies. The dogs had been previously vaccinated against rabies and received booster shots. The individuals were treated for exposure. Both counties have held several free rabies vaccination clinics for pets since the first cases of the local outbreak were discovered in Nassau County in 2024. The cases included four raccoons and two stray cats. A Suffolk County Department of Health Services employee distributes vaccine baits in Suffolk County last month. Credit: Suffolk County Department of Hea In early 2025, as cases started to rise, Nassau County Executive Bruce Blakeman suggested raccoons with rabies may have crossed over from Queens because New York City did not perform rabies vaccine baiting in 2024. The city Health Department said it didn't have the funding that year to continue the program but also said there is no way to determine "how and where rabid raccoons are moving or being moved by people." It also pointed out there was rabies vaccine distribution in southwestern Nassau County in September 2024. Nassau and Suffolk started active surveillance programs, testing raccoons that were found dead or appeared sickly or disoriented. Since raccoons have acclimated well to urban and suburban environments, seeing them during the day foraging for food is common and not a sign they are ill. The number of terrestrial wildlife rabies cases in Nassau jumped to 27 in 2025 — 26 raccoons and one stray cat — and has dropped to one raccoon so far this year. In a statement announcing the latest round of baiting, Nassau Health Commissioner Dr. Irina Gelman called the decline in confirmed rabies cases “encouraging.” “Following the return of rabies in 2024, NCDOH implemented extensive rabies control efforts, including an expanded oral rabies vaccine baiting program to help vaccinate raccoons and other terrestrial wildlife against the virus,” she said. “Those highly successful efforts have continued.” Occasional outbreaks These local outbreaks generally last from one to two years, followed by a decline in the raccoon population due to deaths from the virus, the state Health Department said. The population rebounds in about five years, sometimes causing “periodic flare-ups” of rabies. The dynamics of exposure between humans and raccoons are changing and becoming more challenging, especially with people who feed colonies of stray cats every day, said Gary Whittaker, a professor of virology at the Cornell University College of Veterinary Medicine. Those caretakers are in contact with cats that sometimes end up sharing resources with raccoons — putting them at risk of contracting rabies, he said. “People want to take care of the cats and they put out food for the cats, and the raccoons are taking advantage of that,” he said. “It’s also allowing an increase in the raccoon population.” Whittaker was one of the authors of a study that examined how raccoons in Brooklyn's Green-Wood Cemetery acclimate to the environment and interact with other animals. “They are incredibly successful, smart animals,” he said. “We’ve got pictures of a raccoon and a cat sharing the same food bowl, side by side.” By Lisa L. The outbreak started in Nassau County in 2024 and spread into Suffolk County last year. The latest baiting program was announced in October 2026, with vaccine baits distributed in Suffolk County last month. The publication date is 2026-10-02.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in Long Island, specifically in Suffolk County and Nassau County. The affected population includes raccoons and stray cats, with 51 cases documented in Suffolk County since February 2025, and 27 cases in Nassau County in 2025.
Event
Rabies is spreading among raccoons in Long Island, and health officials are distributing oral rabies vaccine baits to control the outbreak. The Suffolk County Department of Health Services and Nassau County Department of Health have implemented these measures.
Scale, impact and risk
Since February 2025, 51 cases of terrestrial rabies have been documented in Suffolk County, involving 49 raccoons and 2 cats. In Nassau County, the number of cases jumped to 27 in 2025, with 26 raccoons and 1 stray cat. The vaccine baiting program has shown some effectiveness, with 26% of raccoons showing an antibody response. Residents are advised to avoid contact with wildlife and ensure their pets are vaccinated.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health officials are actively distributing vaccine baits and conducting surveillance programs. They are also holding free rabies vaccination clinics for pets. The effectiveness of the program is being monitored, and the last confirmed human rabies case in New York State was over 30 years ago. The source is a full original report, and the information is based on confirmed cases and official statements.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

狂犬病 卷土重来?美国公共卫生基础设施的一个令人担忧的信号

Rabies comeback? A concerning signal for America's public health infrastructure
新闻简报本文讨论了美国 狂犬病 相关查询和 暴露后预防 (PEP) 使用的令人担忧的增长,特别是在 2026 年 7 月至 2026 年 8 月期间。CDC 报告称,狂犬病 查询增加了 17%,PEP 管理增加了 33%,一些州指出了 治疗 中的错误。北卡罗来纳州的一次大规模接触事件涉及宠物动物园的小山羊,促使数百人接受了 PEP。CDC于2026年4月上旬暂停了狂犬病测试,该测试持续了四个月,造成了狂犬病盲点。在此期间,由于缺乏检测,临床医生依靠防御性医学,“以防万一”进行 PEP。测试中断最初被描述为暂时的,是为了质量控制,但恢复测试的确切时间表尚不清楚。CDC 随后发布了 狂犬病 警报,强调需要仔细进行风险评估并遵守 ACIP 指南。该文章提出了机构能力和公众信任度下降对 狂犬病 狂犬病 和 治疗 的影响的担忧,并指出 PEP 使用的增加可能并不反映 狂犬病 病例的实际增加,而是反映了公共卫生基础设施的崩溃。
查看新闻五要素
时间
本文于2026-10-02发表。事件日期范围是2026年7月至8月,其中测试暂停期从2026年4月初开始,持续到2026年4月结束。
地点与对象
这些事件发生在北卡罗来纳州、马里兰州、佛罗里达州和南卡罗来纳州。受影响人群包括通过动物咬伤(尤其是宠物动物园的小山羊)而接触 狂犬病 的人,以及接受 暴露后预防 (PEP) 的个人。
事件
CDC 报告称,2026 年 7 月至 8 月期间,与 狂犬病 相关的询问增加了 17%,PEP 使用和管理错误也有所增加。北卡罗来纳州的一次大规模暴露事件涉及宠物动物园的小山羊,导致数百人接受了 PEP。CDC 还于 2026 年 4 月上旬暂停了 狂犬病 测试,该测试持续了四个月,造成了 狂犬病 盲点。
规模、影响与风险
CDC 收到至少八个州卫生部门的报告,称 狂犬病 PEP 使用量增加、管理错误或两者兼而有之。狂犬病 查询增加了 17%,PEP 管理和 HRIG 使用分别增加了 33% 和 76%。该文章强调了由于测试不可用而过度使用 PEP 的风险以及不必要的 治疗 和成本的可能性。
应对、状态与不确定性
2026 年 4 月恢复测试后,CDC 发布了 狂犬病 警报。据报道,测试中断是暂时的,是为了质量控制,但确切的持续时间和解决方案尚不清楚。文章表明,PEP 使用的增加可能是由于测试暂停,而不是 狂犬病 病例的实际增加,CDC 敦促临床医生遵循基于证据的风险评估。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabies comeback? A concerning signal for America's public health infrastructure

News Brief
The article discusses a concerning rise in rabies-related inquiries and post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) use in the United States, particularly in July through August 2026. The CDC reported a 17% increase in rabies inquiries and a 33% rise in PEP administration, with some states noting errors in treatment. A mass exposure event in North Carolina involved baby goats at a petting zoo, prompting hundreds of people to receive PEP. The CDC paused rabies testing in early April 2026, which lasted for four months, creating a surveillance blind spot. During this period, clinicians relied on defensive medicine, administering PEP "just in case" due to the lack of testing. The testing hiatus was initially described as temporary and for quality control, but the exact timeline for resuming testing was unclear. The CDC later issued a rabies alert, emphasizing the need for careful risk assessments and adherence to ACIP guidelines. The article raises concerns about the impact of reduced agency capacity and public trust on rabies surveillance and treatment, noting that the rise in PEP use may not reflect an actual increase in rabies cases but rather a breakdown in the public health infrastructure.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Search search Link to Facebook page Link to X Link to YouTube channel Link to flipboard Link to RSS Main navigation Home About About ACSH Financials Our Team Video Donate Publications Media/Contact Subscribe Write For Us Rabies comeback? A concerning signal for America's public health infrastructure email facebook twitter linkedin pocket reddit print By Katie Suleta, DHSc, MPH — Oct 02, 2026 Related content: Woman Suffers Terrifying Death from Rabies After Puppy Bite Adopting Stray Cats: What Could Possibly Go Wrong? (Hint: Rabies) A Service Dog Bit My Dad in the Butt. Here's What to Do Next Rabies Information You Can Sink Your Teeth Into Panicked After Touching a Bat? Here's What to Do Eighteen months ago, I asked whether anti-vaccine rhetoric and a retreat from infectious-disease preparedness might open the door for rabies. Now the CDC reports more rabies inquiries, more mass-exposure events, and a sharp rise in lifesaving post-exposure treatment. Is rabies making a comeback or is a strained public-health system making every possible exposure harder to assess? Image by ACSH using AI In February 2025, I wrote about the possibility of rabies making a comeback because of anti-vaccine rhetoric. Eighteen months later, we’ve shifted from a hypothetical warning to an active Health Alert Network advisory from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). “From July through August 2026, CDC received 17% more rabies-related inquiries than during the same period in 2025, including reports of mass exposure events, involving more than one person. During this time frame, CDC also received reports from at least eight state health departments of increased rabies PEP [post-exposure prophylaxis] use, PEP administration errors, or both.” The CDC also tracks rabies vaccine and human rabies immune globulin (HRIG) use. The vaccines are used with HRIG because HRIG acts faster than the vaccine and can bind to the virus quickly. The vaccine then builds longer-term immunity before the virus reaches your nervous system. Similar to the rabies inquiries, the use of the rabies vaccine and HRIG was also tallied for the same time this year as last year. Both also saw sharp increases. This naturally leads to some questions: Are more people being exposed to rabies? Is rabies a bigger problem now than it was this time last year? Rabies Exposure This summer, a mass exposure event in North Carolina involved baby goats at a petting zoo. The goats were part of a traveling petting zoo that made ten stops over a few weeks. This led to a recommendation that hundreds of people receive PEP. Each year, about 100,000 people receive the rabies vaccine. According to the Health Alert Network, vaccinations and HRIG use are up 33% and 76% over the same period last year. Could that be completely due to the rabid baby goats? Probably not. The Health Alert Network also reported rabies inquiries from eight states. For example, there were rabid beaver attacks in Maryland; rabid bats have popped up in Florida several times this year; and rabid raccoons were reported in South Carolina. While animal vectors were clearly active this summer, another major factor driving the spike in rabies PEP treatment wasn't lurking in the woods; it was happening inside CDC laboratories. Rabies Testing To answer whether rabies is actually spreading or if we’re just seeing a surge in defensive treatments, we first have to look at a massive blind spot created by federal testing interruptions. The CDC keeps a running list of tests in its laboratory, including unavailable tests. Stay curious. Stay informed. Free, independent writing delivered weekly — no ads, no sponsored fluff. Join Now Donate In early April 2026, the CDC paused testing for several infectious diseases, including rabies. At the time, the pause was reported as temporary and for quality control purposes. No official word came down on when the tests would be available again, but the implication was that it would be soon. The word “weeks” was thrown around. Four months later, rabies testing became available again, although several other infectious diseases remain on the list. But for four months, the CDC, the agency specifically designed to combat and surveil diseases, couldn’t test for rabies, one of the deadliest infectious diseases. About a month after testing returned, the CDC issued a rabies alert. A 4-month testing hiatus creates a blind spot. When state and local public health departments can't get federal confirmation on suspected cases, clinicians default to defensive medicine, administering expensive PEP "just in case." The testing being unavailable for so long could absolutely have been due to routine maintenance and/or quality control issues. However, this pause is also part of a broader pattern of diminished agency capacity and public erosion of trust under current HHS leadership. This administration has not done itself any favors. Secretary Kennedy, the current HHS secretary, while running for president in 2023, stated, “We’re going to give infectious disease a break for about eight years.” Rabies Vaccines It’s ironic that the health alert also includes the following guidance on rabies. “Although there is currently no shortage of human rabies vaccine or human rabies immunoglobulin in the United States, it is important for clinicians to administer PEP appropriately because errors in rabies risk assessment and PEP administration could result in unnecessary treatment (e.g., additional doses of vaccine or HRIG), potential adverse effects, and avoidable costs. A typical course of PEP can cost between $11,000 and $14,000 per person. Following rabies PEP administration guidance helps ensure that lifesaving vaccines are available for persons who need it and extraneous costs are avoided.” The press release also states that “CDC urges clinicians to conduct careful, evidence-based rabies risk assessments and follow current ACIP [Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices] recommendations when post-exposure vaccination or treatment is indicated.” ACIP guidance hasn’t meaningfully changed since Secretary Kennedy has been at the helm; an important consideration given how much ACIP has changed, with members suddenly ousted, new replacements with questionable credentials, and a notable lack of meetings around fall vaccine recommendations. The question of “would they change the guidance around rabies vaccination recommendations?” is not out of scope here. Rabies is public health 101. Unlike novel viruses or contested chronic illnesses, the course of rabies is absolute: if you become symptomatic, you die. But if you get PEP on time, you live. No middle ground or political debate exists on the biology. Microbes do not take sabbaticals for political cycles. Infectious diseases don't pause because leadership wants to shift attention to wellness trends or alter vaccine schedules. The "break" from infectious disease didn't stop animal vectors or rabid petting zoo goats. It just broke the surveillance infrastructure designed to protect us from them. Rabies is a harbinger. If the public health apparatus is fumbling risk assessment, surveillance, and basic clinical administration on a disease that is 100% fatal yet completely preventable, it signals a breakdown in our core public health infrastructure. If rabid baby goats aren’t enough to make you reconsider your stance on the importance of vaccines and infectious diseases, likely nothing will. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

英国 狂犬病 疫苗 试验也可以解锁针对 X 疾病的保护 - CEPI

UK rabies vaccine trial could also unlock protection against Disease X - CEPI
新闻简报一项由伦敦帝国学院和切尔西和温莎医院 NHS 基础信托联合领导的英国试验正在开展,该试验针对一种名为 RAB-Vac 的候选疫苗 狂犬病 疫苗,由 CEPI 提供资金支持。该试验涉及 48 名 18 至 55 岁的健康成年人,评估该 疫苗 的安全性及 免疫应答。该 疫苗 采用新一代 RNA 技术,这种技术有可能被重新用于快速应对未来的流行病威胁,包括一种假设性的 'Disease X'。狂犬病 会导致神经系统症状,一旦进入大脑几乎总是致命,每年估计导致约 60,000 人死亡,儿童尤其容易受到感染。该试验是 CEPI 更广泛计划的一部分,旨在通过 RNA 基础平台的速度和灵活性,在 100 天内开发 疫苗 以应对新的传染病。该研究得到了 NIHR 帝国生物医学研究中心的支持,旨在展示该平台在应对新兴威胁时快速、可扩展的 疫苗 潜力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
The trial began with the first participants being vaccinated on 2026-09-29, as reported in the article. The trial is ongoing and will continue to assess the vaccine candidate's safety and immune response.
地点与对象
该试验在英国伦敦切尔西和威斯敏斯特医院进行,涉及约 48 名年龄在 18 岁至 55 岁之间的健康成年参与者,他们可以接受所有随访。
事件
英国帝国理工学院、切尔西和威斯敏斯特医院 NHS 基金会信托基金正在对 狂犬病 疫苗(称为 RAB-Vac)进行研究,并得到 CEPI 的资助。该试验旨在评估疫苗和免疫应答的安全性,它基于下一代RNA技术。
规模、影响与风险
试验涉及48名参与者,尽管文章没有报告任何病例、死亡或暴露情况,但它突出了疫苗在应对狂犬病方面的潜力,这是一种会引起神经系统症状并且一旦传播到大脑几乎总是导致死亡的疾病。狂犬病每年估计导致60,000人死亡,儿童尤其容易受到感染。
应对、状态与不确定性
试验正在进行中,第一批参与者已接种疫苗。CEPI 和伦敦帝国理工学院致力于确保低收入和中等收入国家能够负担得起基于该技术开发的 疫苗。该试验是针对未来流行病威胁(包括 X 病)开发快速反应 疫苗 的更广泛努力的一部分。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

UK rabies vaccine trial could also unlock protection against Disease X - CEPI

News Brief
A UK trial of an investigational rabies vaccine, known as RAB-Vac, is underway, led by Imperial College London and Chelsea and Westminster Hospital NHS Foundation Trust, with funding from CEPI. The trial involves 48 healthy adult participants aged 18-55, who are being assessed for the vaccine's safety and immune response. The vaccine uses next-generation RNA technology, which could potentially be repurposed to rapidly respond to future pandemic threats, including a hypothetical 'Disease X'. Rabies, which causes neurological symptoms and is almost always fatal once it reaches the brain, kills an estimated 60,000 people annually, with children being particularly vulnerable. The trial is part of CEPI's broader initiative to develop vaccines against new infectious diseases within 100 days, leveraging the speed and flexibility of RNA-based platforms. The study is supported by the NIHR Imperial Biomedical Research Centre and aims to demonstrate the platform's potential for rapid, scalable vaccine responses to emerging threats.
Original source text (English)
Securing the future: CEPI's 3.0 Investment Case Find out moreDismiss About What we do Partner with us Our impact News & stories Contact us News UK rabies vaccine trial could also unlock protection against Disease X Jodie Rogers29th September 2026 Share The first participants have been vaccinated in a new UK trial of an investigational rabies vaccine that could be easier and cheaper to produce than existing vaccines,while also providing crucial insights into whether the vaccine’s underlying next-generation RNA technology can help rapidly respond to future pandemic threats. The first-in-human study, known as RAB-Vac, is led in collaboration between Imperial College London and Chelsea and Westminster Hospital NHS Foundation Trust and funded by the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI). Approximately 48 healthy adult participants will take part in the research department at Chelsea and Westminster Hospital in London to assess the safety of the vaccine candidate and the immune response generated following vaccination. People become ill with rabies after being bitten or scratched by infected animals, with stray dogs accounting for the majority of infections worldwide. If the illness reaches the brain, it results in neurological symptoms and, almost always, death. An estimated 60,000 people lose their lives to the disease each year, almost one person every nine minutes – with children particularly vulnerable - and the number of infections is increasing in parts of Asia and Africa. While rabies vaccines are licensed for use in people, current designs are difficult and expensive to manufacture which can limit getting supplies to where they are needed most. The investigational vaccine being tested in RAB-Vac could mean the vaccine is easier to make at a lower cost. As the vaccine candidate uses an innovative fast and flexible RNA design, it could also unlock clues on the potential to repurpose the underlying platform to rapidly fend off infectious diseases with epidemic or pandemic potential, including a new or as-yet-identified ‘Disease X’. “We don’t know when the next Disease X is coming – nor what it may be - but we know it’s guaranteed,” explains Dr Kent Kester, Executive Director of Research and Development, CEPI. “We therefore need to leverage the world’s leading vaccine innovations, like the RNA technology Imperial College London is developing, to be ready to rapidly respond to whatever may come our way.” mRNA technology became a pivotal part of the COVID-19 pandemic response, quickly producing safe and highly effective COVID-19 vaccines in record time. Its plug-and-play design means the technology can be quickly adapted to code for vaccines against other pathogens. As every day counts in response to an outbreak, this speed of development could be vital, supporting CEPI’s goal to create vaccines against new threats within 100 days and giving the world the chance to prevent a future pandemic. Imperial’s vaccine candidate also holds potential in helping to respond to a future outbreak as it uses a self-amplifying mRNA approach. In addition to containing genetic instructions that safely code for immune-generating antigens, as is seen in conventional mRNA designs, the vaccine platform also contains the instructions for replicase, an enzyme that serves as a built-in photocopier temporarily teaching the body how to make more mRNA. This self-copying design could help to reduce the dose of RNA needed while maintaining the effectiveness of the vaccine which could help to extend supply of a vaccine to reach more people in an outbreak. “This marks a significant milestone in our partnership with CEPI and in the development of a next-generation self-amplifying RNA vaccine platform capable of delivering protection at lower doses than conventional mRNA vaccines” explains Professor Robin Shattock, Head of Mucosal Infection and Immunity within the Department of Medicine at Imperial College London and lead of the study. “The clinical evaluation of our rabies vaccine is an important step in demonstrating the platform’s potential to support rapid, scalable vaccine responses to future pandemic threats.” CEPI has been supporting the development of Imperial College London’s self-amplifying RNA vaccine since 2018, providing up to £11.1 million into researching the technology. This included funding to a similar RNA platform evaluated during COVID-19 which demonstrated a favourable safety profile in more than 200 participants. Both CEPI and Imperial College London are committed to ensuring any vaccines developed on the CEPI-supported technology are made affordable for low- and middle-income countries during an outbreak, if they are successful in testing. This study has received infrastructure support in part by the NIHR Imperial Biomedical Research Centre (BRC). For more information on the trial and signing up to take part as a volunteer, visit the Imperial Medicine or Chelsea and Westminster Hospital Foundation NHS Trust websites. Participants must meet certain eligibility criteria, including being aged 18-55 and availability for all follow-up visits. Time
The trial began with the first participants being vaccinated on 2026-09-29, as reported in the article. The trial is ongoing and will continue to assess the vaccine candidate's safety and immune response.
Location and population
The trial is taking place at Chelsea and Westminster Hospital in London, UK, involving approximately 48 healthy adult participants aged 18-55 who are available for all follow-up visits.
Event
A UK trial of an investigational rabies vaccine, known as RAB-Vac, is being conducted by Imperial College London and Chelsea and Westminster Hospital NHS Foundation Trust, with funding from CEPI. The trial aims to assess the vaccine's safety and immune response, and it is based on next-generation RNA technology.
Scale, impact and risk
The trial involves 48 participants, and while the article does not report any cases, deaths, or exposures, it highlights the potential of the vaccine to address rabies, a disease that causes neurological symptoms and almost always results in death if it reaches the brain. Rabies is estimated to kill 60,000 people annually, with children being particularly vulnerable.
Response, status and uncertainty
The trial is in progress, with the first participants vaccinated. CEPI and Imperial College London are committed to ensuring affordability of vaccines developed on this technology for low- and middle-income countries. The trial is part of a broader effort to develop rapid-response vaccines against future pandemic threats, including Disease X.
