B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

世界 狂犬病 日:了解这种疾病 - The Hans India

World Rabies Day: Know About This Disease - The Hans India
新闻简报本文讨论了每年 9 月 28 日举行的世界 狂犬病 日,以纪念路易·巴斯德 (Louis Pasteur) 对 狂犬病 疫苗 的贡献。它强调了卫生当局在全球范围内努力向公众宣传 狂犬病(一种由狂犬病病毒引起的危及生命的病毒性疾病)。狂犬病 主要通过狗咬伤传播,占感染的 99%,也可以通过受感染动物的唾液通过破损的皮肤进入体内传播。文章指出,每年约有 59,000 人死于 狂犬病,其中大部分在亚洲和非洲。它强调了被咬伤后立即就医的重要性,包括用肥皂和水彻底清洗伤口、使用消毒剂和暴露后预防,其中可能涉及狂犬病免疫球蛋白和疫苗。文章还提到,一旦出现攻击性、流涎过多和恐水症等症状,狂犬病 几乎总是致命的。它强调了及时采取 治疗 和提高预防疾病传播意识的必要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
世界 狂犬病 日于每年 9 月 28 日举行。路易斯·巴斯德 (Louis Pasteur) 于 1895 年 9 月 28 日去世。狂犬病 的症状可能会在感染发生几天甚至几年后出现。
地点与对象
全球庆祝世界 狂犬病 日。狂犬病 是一种病毒感染,可以从动物传播给人类,特别是通过狗咬伤,高达 99% 的 狂犬病 感染都是由狗咬伤引起的。
事件
世界 狂犬病 日是一项年度活动,于 9 月 28 日举行,旨在向公众宣传 狂犬病 及其原因、迹象和预防措施。狂犬病 是一种由狂犬病病毒引起的危及生命的病毒性疾病,一旦症状开始出现,几乎总是致命的。
规模、影响与风险
据多种消息来源称,每年约有 59,000 人因 狂犬病 感染而死亡,其中大部分发生在亚洲和非洲大陆。狂犬病 可以通过狗、猫和啮齿动物等宠物传播,被狗或任何其他动物咬伤后,无论 狂犬病 是什么,都需要去看医生。
应对、状态与不确定性
世界各地的卫生当局和组织在世界 狂犬病 日向公众宣传 狂犬病。医生确定个体是否需要 暴露后预防 而非 狂犬病,其中包括施用 狂犬病 免疫球蛋白和 疫苗。就诊前,应使用肥皂和流水彻底清洗伤口不少于 15 分钟,并使用聚维酮碘等消毒液。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

World Rabies Day: Know About This Disease - The Hans India

News Brief
The article discusses World Rabies Day, observed annually on 28 September to honor Louis Pasteur's contributions to the rabies vaccine. It highlights the global efforts of health authorities to educate the public about rabies, a life-threatening viral disease caused by Lyssavirus. Rabies is primarily transmitted through dog bites, which account for up to 99% of infections, and can also spread via an infected animal's saliva entering the body through broken skin. The article notes that about 59,000 people die from rabies each year, mostly in Asia and Africa. It emphasizes the importance of immediate medical attention after a bite, including thorough washing of the wound with soap and water, application of antiseptic, and post-exposure prophylaxis, which may involve rabies immunoglobulin and vaccine. The article also mentions that rabies is almost always fatal once symptoms appear, which include aggression, excessive salivation, and hydrophobia. It underscores the need for prompt treatment and awareness to prevent the spread of the disease.
Original source text (English)
The World Rabies Day is held on 28th September annually as tribute to the contributions made by Louis Pasteur in creating the first rabies vaccine. World Rabies Day: Know About This Disease On World Rabies Day , which falls on 28 September, health authorities and organisations across the world educate the public about the causes, signs, and prevention of rabies. In addition, on this day, schools conduct rabies education programmes to increase understanding of the disease and promote prevention measures. According to various sources, about 59,000 people die due to rabies infection every year, mostly in Asian and African continents. The World Rabies Day is held on 28th September annually as tribute to the contributions made by Louis Pasteur in creating the first rabies vaccine. The occasion highlights the end of such dangerous treatments that are done locally against rabies infection, for example, beating of the dog bite wound by slipper. Furthermore, the World Rabies Day intends to create awareness about rabies, a life-threatening viral disease caused by Lyssavirus. Louis Pasteur was born on 27 December 1822 in Dole, France. This Noble Prize winner in the field of chemistry and biology identified microorganisms responsible for causing many diseases and developed vaccines against the diseases. His father was a leather businessman and painter. Some of his paintings are kept at the Pasteur Museum, even those of his kids. Louis Pasteur died on 28 September 1895 and was buried in the crypt of the Pasteur Institute in Paris. Only a few French people have the privilege of being laid to rest in the same place. Rabies is a viral infection that can be transmitted from an animal to a human through a bite wound. Additionally, it can also be contracted if an infected animal’s saliva gets into the body through broken skin. For instance, the disease can be transmitted from pets such as dogs, cats, and rodents. However, a dog’s bite causes up to 99% of rabies infections. Furthermore, the virus that causes rabies has the ability to enter the central nervous system via nerves at the spot of the dog bite. Additionally, while the chances of infection from animals that have been vaccinated are minimal, it is still possible. Therefore, a bite from a dog or any other animal requires one to visit a doctor irrespective of vaccination. Rabies is almost always lethal once the symptoms begin to manifest. Also, the symptoms may arise days or even years after the infection has taken place. A person that is infected will suffer from aggression, excessive salivation, and difficulty in swallowing. Furthermore, the person may also develop hydrophobia which is fear of water. When one is bitten by an animal, the wound should be washed thoroughly for not less than 15 minutes using soap and running water. In addition, antiseptic solution such as povidone-iodine should be applied to the bite before the individual visits the hospital. A medical practitioner determines whether the individual requires post exposure prophylaxis against rabies. Prophylaxis entails the administration of rabies immunoglobulin and rabies vaccine. In addition, tetanus toxoid and other antibiotics might be administered during the process. One who has suffered from the bite should not use acid or any other substance on the wound and should not have it exposed to any dirty hands or delay going for the treatment. A healthcare provider will also determine if the wound needs to be stitched or not depending on its gravity. In certain circumstances, pre-exposure prophylaxis against rabies might be suggested by the healthcare provider to individuals at high risk. Whether the injection will be provided depends on the type of exposure, the species of the animal, and the immunization status of the patient. The World Rabies Day is held on 28th September annually. Louis Pasteur died on 28 September 1895. The symptoms of rabies may arise days or even years after the infection has taken place.
Location and population
World Rabies Day is observed globally. Rabies is a viral infection that can be transmitted from animals to humans, particularly through dog bites, which cause up to 99% of rabies infections.
Event
World Rabies Day is an annual event held on 28 September to educate the public about rabies, its causes, signs, and prevention. Rabies is a life-threatening viral disease caused by Lyssavirus, and it is almost always lethal once symptoms begin to manifest.
Scale, impact and risk
According to various sources, about 59,000 people die due to rabies infection every year, mostly in Asian and African continents. Rabies can be transmitted from pets such as dogs, cats, and rodents, and a bite from a dog or any other animal requires one to visit a doctor irrespective of vaccination.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health authorities and organisations across the world educate the public about rabies on World Rabies Day. A medical practitioner determines whether the individual requires post-exposure prophylaxis against rabies, which includes the administration of rabies immunoglobulin and vaccine. The wound should be washed thoroughly for not less than 15 minutes using soap and running water, and antiseptic solution such as povidone-iodine should be applied before visiting the hospital.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Harford County records 28 rabies cases as low-cost vaccine clinic planned - WMAR 2 News Baltimore

News Brief
Harford County, Maryland, has recorded 28 confirmed rabies cases, matching the total from last year. An infected cat was found at the Humane Society, prompting officials to emphasize the importance of vaccination. In response, the Humane Society is organizing a low-cost vaccine clinic on October 24 at the Aberdeen Fire Department. This initiative aims to make rabies vaccinations more accessible, especially for pet owners facing financial challenges. Rabies vaccines are typically expensive, but the clinic offers them at a fraction of the cost, potentially saving hundreds of dollars. Officials warn that once symptoms appear, it is too late to treat rabies, underscoring the urgency of prevention. The event is part of ongoing efforts to combat the outbreak and protect both pets and humans from the deadly disease.

FALLSTON, Md. — The list reads like a virtual ‘Who’s Who’ of local wildlife, 28 confirmed cases of rabies matching last year’s entire total in Harford County, and recently, an infected, stray cat turned up at the Humane Society. “It was like the classic like foaming and head tilt,” explained Marketing Director Erin Long. Rabies vaccines are mandated for domestic animals, and Long says in the absence of the vaccination, contracting rabies can mean certain death. “It’s too late,” Long told us, “Once they start showing signs and symptoms, it’s too late and, yes, the only way to test is by taking a tissue sample from the brain. So yes, it’s too late.” WATCH: Protecting your pet from rabies Low-cost vaccine clinic planned amid rabies concerns in Harford County In a bid to protect pets, the Humane Society will be holding a vaccine clinic on Saturday, Oct. 24 at the Aberdeen Fire Department on North Rogers Street. Workers at a similar event in the spring vaccinated almost 500 animals, and they’re expecting even more in light of the rabies outbreak. They’re also offering the vaccines at pennies on the dollar compared to the shots offered by most veterinarians, which can cost a few hundred bucks. “People are struggling right now,” said Long, “When it’s a case of, ‘Am I going to, you know, feed myself? Am I going to take care of my pet?’ And oftentimes, care for the pet does start to suffer, so we are trying to keep our price point really low. So, yes, $15 vaccinations. It’s really unheard of.” This is a developing story, so check back with WMAR2News.com for updates. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Clark County reports human cases of West Nile, animal case of rabies - WYSO Public Radio

News Brief
Clark County, Ohio, reported two confirmed cases of infectious diseases on October 1, 2026: a human case of West Nile virus and a rabies case in a bat. The human case was identified on the southern side of Elmwood Place in Springfield, while the rabid bat was found in Medway, Bethel Township. These cases are the first of their kind in the county since 2021. West Nile virus is spread by infected mosquitoes, and rabies can be transmitted to humans through animal bites. The Clark County Combined Health District emphasized that West Nile virus does not spread between humans, but rabies is a severe illness with potentially fatal outcomes if untreated. To prevent mosquito-borne diseases, residents are advised to use EPA-approved insect repellent, wear long sleeves and pants, and eliminate standing water around homes. The health district is conducting an investigation into the cases and has not reported any further details on the spread or impact of these diseases.

Social Media Discussion Policy Diversity, Equity and Inclusion FY25 Impact Report 2022-2026 Strategic Plan WYSO Station Updates Local & Statewide News Clark County reports human cases of West Nile, animal case of rabies WYSO | By Shay Frank Published October 1, 2026 at 2:26 PM EDT Facebook LinkedIn Email CDC / Public Domain Mosquitoes spread the West Nile virus. Clark County is investigating two infectious diseases including a human case of West Nile virus and a case of rabies in a bat. The human West Nile virus case is on the southern side of Elmwood Place in Springfield, while the rabid bat was found in the village of Medway in Bethel Township. Both cases of infectious disease are making their first appearance since 2021 in the county. West Nile virus is spread by infected mosquitoes that bite humans and rabies can be transmitted to humans by a variety of animals. Bats are the most common carriers of rabies and the only animals who have tested positive for rabies in Clark County in the last 20 years. West Nile virus cannot spread from person to person. The illness spread by mosquito also often goes undetected as 80% of infected people show no symptoms. If symptoms do show up for those exposed, they typically onset within three to fourteen days. Those include severe or mild fever, inflammation of the brain, head and body aches, vomiting, diarrhea, rash and swollen glands. Mild symptoms can last for a few days to a week. The Clark County Combined Health District said the best way to protect yourself is to prevent mosquito bites with EPA registered insect repellent. When outdoors, residents are encouraged to also wear loose-fitting, long sleeves and pants to protect from exposure. Residents can also prevent mosquitos around their home by emptying or covering items that hold water like buckets, tires, flowerpots, birdbaths and trash containers. If they cannot be emptied or discarded, residents can use products containing Bacillus thuringiensis israelensis to control mosquito larvae. the product is available at many garden and home improvement stores, About one in 150 people infected with the virus develop severe illness. Those symptoms include as high fever, headache, neck stiffness, muscle weakness, disorientation, tremors, seizures, paralysis, and coma. For rabies, exposure can come from a bite, scratch, direct skin contact with an infected animal and even sharing a room with that animal. Pet owners are encouraged to vaccinate their dogs and cats for rabies and keep domestic animals clear of all wildlife. If a bat is found near an unattended child or someone unable to communicate if exposure has occurred, exposure may have still happened. The resident who discovered the infected bat in Clark County has received life-saving post exposure prophylaxis. While rabies is rare in humans, it is always fatal once symptoms arise. If exposure has happened immediate medical care is critical. Officials said it is always best to capture the bat for testing but not to touch it with bare hands. People and pets should be kept away from the animal and doors or windows should be closed to limit escape. Once a bat is found and captured, residents should contact Clark County Health for guidance on testing it for rabies. The health department can be reached at 937-390-5600 or at info@clarkhealthohio.gov. Tags Local & Statewide News Clark County Combined Health District Facebook LinkedIn Email Shay Frank Shay Frank (she/her) was born and raised in Dayton. Published on 2026-10-01. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the cases are reported as making their first appearance since 2021 in the county.
Location and population
Clark County, specifically the southern side of Elmwood Place in Springfield for the human West Nile virus case and the village of Medway in Bethel Township for the rabid bat case. The affected population includes humans and bats.
Event
Clark County reports a human case of West Nile virus and an animal case of rabies. These cases are confirmed by the Clark County Combined Health District.
Scale, impact and risk
Two confirmed cases: one human West Nile virus case and one rabid bat case. The risk of human-to-human transmission for West Nile virus is low, as it cannot spread from person to person. Rabies is a severe illness with potentially fatal outcomes if not treated promptly.
Response, status and uncertainty
Clark County is investigating the cases. The health district has issued prevention guidelines, including using EPA registered insect repellent, wearing protective clothing, and eliminating standing water to prevent mosquito breeding.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

狂犬病 正在崛起:专家提供建议

Rabies Is on the Rise: Experts Offer Advice
新闻简报该文章报告称,全国范围内 狂犬病 暴露查询数量有所增加,CDC 指出,与 2025 年同期相比,2026 年 7 月至 8 月增加了 17%。专家强调,如果管理不当,狂犬病 几乎总是致命的,而 暴露后预防 (PEP) 至关重要。PEP 涉及四剂 疫苗,并且可能需要注射破伤风疫苗,具体取决于咬伤的严重程度。密歇根州法律要求立即向当地卫生部门报告动物咬伤事件。虽然松鼠和兔子等啮齿动物不太可能携带 狂犬病,但被未接种疫苗的宠物或蝙蝠等动物咬伤可能需要 PEP。通常需要对动物进行专门测试来确认 狂犬病,尽管一些线索(例如异常行为)可能会有所帮助。该文章强调了咨询公共卫生官员以获取关于是否需要 PEP 的指导的重要性,特别是在涉及蝙蝠或未接种疫苗的动物的情况下。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年10月2日(发布日期);2026 年 7 月至 8 月(活动日期)。
地点与对象
美国;一般人群,特别是接触蝙蝠或动物的人群。
事件
CDC 注意到,全国范围内 狂犬病 接触报告有所增加,与 2025 年同期相比,询问量增加了 17%。专家建议在接触蝙蝠或动物咬伤后寻求医疗救助。
规模、影响与风险
狂犬病 相关查询增加 17%;如果管理不当,狂犬病 是 100% 致命的;对于未接种疫苗的宠物或可能被感染的动物的咬伤,需要进行 PEP。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心发布健康咨询;密歇根州法律要求报告动物咬伤事件;建议针对潜在的 狂犬病 暴露进行 PEP;需要专门的测试来确认动物中的 狂犬病。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabies Is on the Rise: Experts Offer Advice

News Brief
The article reports a nationwide increase in rabies exposure inquiries, with the CDC noting a 17% rise from July to August 2026 compared to the same period in 2025. Experts emphasize that rabies is nearly always fatal if not managed properly, and post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) is crucial. PEP involves four vaccine doses and may require a tetanus shot, depending on the bite's severity. Michigan law mandates immediate reporting of animal bites to local health departments. While rodents like squirrels and rabbits are unlikely to carry rabies, bites from unvaccinated pets or animals like bats may necessitate PEP. Specialized testing of the animal is typically required to confirm rabies, though some clues, such as unusual behavior, may help. The article highlights the importance of consulting public health officials for guidance on whether PEP is needed, especially in cases involving bats or unvaccinated animals.
Original source text (English)

Forgotten Password Cancel Home / Featured Content / Rabies Is on the Rise: Experts Offer Advice Rabies Is on the Rise: Experts Offer Advice Infectious disease experts weigh in on what to do if you think you’ve been exposed to rabies October 2, 2026 by University of Michigan Health Leave a Comment By Kelly Malcom Picture it: it’s the middle of the night and you’ve suddenly woken up to the sound of flapping wings. Somehow a bat has made its way into your home. Do you know what to do next? The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recently issued a health advisory for clinicians noting a nationwide increase in rabies exposure reports. According to the report, from July through August 2026, CDC received 17% more rabies-related inquiries than during the same period in 2025. “Rabies is an infection of the brain that is lethal in most mammals, including humans,” said Adam Lauring., M.D., Ph.D., head of the Division of Infectious Disease at Michigan Medicine, who recently had his own nighttime encounter with a bat. “Infected animals shed virus in their saliva, and it gains entry through breaks in skin or direct contact with mucus membranes. The virus then gains access to nerves and travels along the nerves toward the brain.” People are usually exposed via bites from infected animals, such as bats, raccoons, skunks or unvaccinated pets. Because there’s no treatment for rabies, management is focused on interrupting the virus’ ability to infiltrate the nervous system, because once that happens, it’s too late. Rabies “is 100% fatal – or nearly so – if the exposure is not managed appropriately,” said Lauring. Batty behavior “If you wake up with a bat in your room, you can’t rule out a bite or scratch while you were sleeping (even if you don’t see one). So, you should contact the health department and/or animal control for trapping/testing/or recommendations,” said Lauring. “In my case, I was so freaked out, I opened the window and ran out of the room, so we couldn’t test the bat. So, I needed to proceed with post exposure prophylaxis,” he said. The good news is you have some time after an animal exposure, says Laraine Washer, M.D., who consulted with Lauring following his encounter. “In most cases, rabies post-exposure prophylaxis, known as PEP, is a medical urgency, not an emergency. You can normally wait 48-72 hours to determine whether an animal is available for testing or observation.” Unfortunately, you can’t always rely on an animal’s behavior to tell whether it’s infected with rabies. “Specialized testing of the animal in a public health laboratory is typically required to determine if it had rabies.” But there can be some clues, including daytime activity in a nocturnal animal or an animal attacking without provocation, says Lauring. You’ve been bitten by an animal. What do you do? If you are bitten, wash the wound with soap and water and seek medical care. Depending on the severity and type of bite, a visit to urgent care or an emergency department may be required, says Washer, for a tetanus shot and depending on rabies reports in your area, rabies immunoglobulin and the first dose of rabies vaccine. Rabies PEP includes four total visits for vaccine doses. Michigan law requires that animal bites are immediately reported to the local health department. Thankfully, rodents like squirrels, hamsters, mice, and rabbits are unlikely to have rabies in the United States and public health officials are unlikely to recommend PEP for those types of bites. Whether rabies prophylaxis treatment is required for bites from pets, such as dogs, cats, and ferrets depends on whether they’ve been vaccinated. “Clinicians should consult with local or state public health officials when there are questions about whether PEP is indicated,” said Washer. Bottom line is when in doubt, say Michigan Medicine experts, seek medical attention following exposure to bats or when bitten by an animal. Sign up for Health Lab newsletters today. 杭州研究发现,老年人在被动物咬伤后推迟寻求 狂犬病 治疗

Older Adults Delay Seeking Rabies Treatment After Animal Bites, Hangzhou Study Finds
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 2 日发表在《BMC Infectiousdies》上的一项研究发现,与年轻人相比,中国杭州的老年人在被动物咬伤后更有可能推迟寻求 狂犬病 治疗。这项由同德医院郑阳领导的研究分析了 2022 年 10 月至 2025 年 10 月期间的 3,327 例临床就诊。研究使用基于日期的延迟代理,发现 6.6% 的病例在随后的日历日就诊。老年人延迟就诊的调整后优势比为 2.28,这表明他们延迟就诊的可能性是老年人的两倍多。该研究还指出,老年人不太可能进行院前伤口自我 治疗,尽管这一发现在统计上并不显着。作者强调,数据无法确定这些行为是否存在关联,他们呼吁开展有针对性的公共卫生活动,以解决老年人寻求护理行为的这一差距。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2026年10月2日发表,分析的数据跨度为2022年10月至2025年10月。事件日期并未明确说明,但研究结果基于该期间记录的临床表现。
地点与对象
该研究在中国杭州进行,重点关注 3,327 名 队列研究 患者,这些患者在潜在的 狂犬病 暴露后到诊所就诊。感兴趣的人群包括 65 岁及以上的老年人。
事件
一项来自中国杭州的回顾性研究发表在《BMC传染病》上,发现与年轻人相比,65岁及以上的老年人在被动物咬伤后更有可能推迟寻求狂犬病 治疗。这种延迟是通过在随后的日历日而不是受伤当天就诊来衡量的。该研究还检查了院前伤口处理,发现自我 治疗 在老年人中较少见,尽管没有统计学意义。
规模、影响与风险
在 3,327 例临床表现中,218 例 (6.6%) 发生在受伤后的第二天。老年人延迟就诊的调整后优势比为 2.28,表明他们延迟就诊的可能性是老年人的两倍多。该研究强调了由于延迟护理而导致可预防死亡的风险,特别是考虑到 狂犬病病毒 能够沿着神经传播,并且一天的延迟可能会影响结果。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究使用电子病历进行,并采用严格的排除标准。该研究结果基于回顾性单中心分析,该分析在捕获所有暴露个体方面存在局限性。作者警告说,老年人自我 治疗 的结果并不精确,需要进一步确认。该研究的设计和统计报告提供了可信度,但数据无法确定延迟和自我 治疗 行为是否在同一个体中存在关联。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Older Adults Delay Seeking Rabies Treatment After Animal Bites, Hangzhou Study Finds

News Brief
A study published in BMC Infectious Diseases on October 2, 2026, found that older adults in Hangzhou, China, are more likely to delay seeking rabies treatment after animal bites compared to younger adults. The research, led by Zheng Yang at Tongde Hospital, analyzed 3,327 clinic presentations between October 2022 and October 2025. Using a date-based proxy for delay, the study found that 6.6% of cases were presented on a subsequent calendar day. Older adults had an adjusted odds ratio of 2.28 for delayed presentation, suggesting they are more than twice as likely to delay care. The study also noted that older adults were less likely to perform pre-hospital wound self-treatment, though this finding was not statistically significant. The authors emphasized that the data cannot determine if these behaviors are linked, and they called for targeted public health campaigns to address this gap in care-seeking behavior among older adults.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, October 3, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Older Adults Delay Seeking Rabies Treatment After Animal Bites, Hangzhou Study Finds by Bioengineer October 2, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Rabies remains one of the deadliest infectious diseases known to medicine, with a case fatality rate that approaches 100 percent once clinical symptoms appear. Yet the disease is almost entirely preventable if exposed individuals receive timely post-exposure prophylaxis, a regimen of vaccination and, in higher-risk cases, immunoglobulin administration that can stop the virus before it reaches the nervous system. A new retrospective study from Hangzhou, China, published in BMC Infectious Diseases, has now added an important piece to the puzzle of why some people fail to access this lifesaving care promptly, and the answer points squarely at age. Researchers led by Zheng Yang of the Emergency Department at Tongde Hospital of Zhejiang Province, affiliated with Zhejiang Chinese Medical University, analyzed 3,327 exposure-related clinic presentations recorded between October 2022 and October 2025. The team began with a larger pool of records and applied rigorous exclusion criteria, removing seven system-test entries, fifteen exposures classified as World Health Organization Category I, thirteen confirmed continuation visits, ten records with contradictory dates, and two unresolved duplicates. What remained was a clean hospital-based cohort suitable for examining two distinct questions: how quickly patients presented after a potential rabies exposure, and whether they had attempted any wound care on their own before arriving at the clinic. The central finding concerns timing. Of the 3,327 presentations, 218, or 6.6 percent, occurred on a calendar day after the day of injury. Because the study relied on electronic medical records rather than precise timestamps, the researchers used a date-based operational proxy for delay: presenting on a subsequent calendar day rather than the same day. This is a conservative measure, and the authors are careful to frame it as such, but it captures a meaningful gap in access to care. Rabies virus travels along peripheral nerves at a relatively slow rate, and every hour of delay before prophylaxis begins extends the window during which the virus could, in principle, establish itself at the wound site and begin its ascent. When the researchers fitted multivariable logistic regression models adjusting for other characteristics, patients aged 65 and older had significantly higher adjusted odds of subsequent-calendar-day presentation compared with the reference group aged 15 to 44 years. The adjusted odds ratio was 2.28, with a 95 percent confidence interval of 1.31 to 3.96 and a P value of 0.003. In practical terms, older adults in this cohort were more than twice as likely to show up at the clinic only after the calendar day of their exposure had passed. This age-related pattern persisted even after accounting for other variables in the model, suggesting that advancing age itself, or factors closely tied to it, creates a genuine barrier to prompt care-seeking after an animal bite or scratch. The study also examined pre-hospital wound management, the self-directed cleaning or treatment that patients perform before reaching professional care. Guidelines from the World Health Organization emphasize immediate and thorough washing of bite wounds with soap and running water for at least fifteen minutes, a simple intervention that mechanically reduces viral load at the exposure site. The researchers modeled any pre-hospital wound self-treatment as a separate binary outcome. Interestingly, the estimate for the oldest age group pointed in the opposite direction: patients aged 65 and older appeared less likely to report any self-treatment, with an adjusted odds ratio of 0.71 and a confidence interval of 0.50 to 1.01. However, this estimate did not reach the conventional threshold of statistical significance, with a P value of 0.056, and the authors explicitly caution that it is imprecise and requires confirmation in future work. A methodologically important point in the paper is that the two outcomes were modeled separately. The authors stress that their analysis does not establish a combined individual-level outcome linking delay and self-treatment within the same patient. In other words, the data cannot tell us whether an older adult who delays presentation is also the same person who skips wound washing, or whether these behaviors occur independently across the cohort. This distinction matters for intervention design: if delay and inadequate wound care cluster in the same individuals, targeted outreach could address both at once, whereas independent patterns might require broader, population-level messaging. Two other associations emerged from the models. Patients who reported any pre-hospital wound self-treatment had higher adjusted odds of subsequent-calendar-day presentation, with an adjusted odds ratio of 2.72 and a 95 percent confidence interval of 2.00 to 3.69. One plausible interpretation is that people who attempt self-care may believe they have managed the problem adequately and therefore postpone professional evaluation, though the observational design of the study prevents any firm causal claim. The temporal pattern of the data also revealed that presentations during the clinic-opening calendar period of October through December 2022 were associated with subsequent-calendar-day presentation, with an adjusted odds ratio of 2.54. The authors interpret this finding as exploratory, noting that it may reflect the establishment phase of the clinic itself, shifts in case mix, seasonality of animal exposures, or unmeasured access factors rather than any stable behavioral pattern. The implications for rabies control programs, particularly in China where dog-mediated rabies persists at low but nonzero levels, are significant. Older adults represent a vulnerable population on multiple fronts. They may have reduced mobility, limited awareness of modern PEP protocols, greater reliance on traditional remedies, or fewer social contacts who can urge them to seek care quickly. Sensory and cognitive changes can also affect how seriously a minor bite wound is perceived. Because the incubation period of rabies can range from weeks to months depending on the site and severity of exposure, a delay of even one day is unlikely to be decisive in every case, but the cumulative effect of late presentation across a population contributes to preventable deaths. Public health campaigns that specifically target older adults, perhaps through community health workers, primary care touchpoints, or family-centered messaging, could help close this gap. The study’s design has limitations that the authors acknowledge. As a retrospective, single-center, hospital-based analysis, it captures only people who eventually reached the clinic, leaving unexamined the experiences of exposed individuals who never sought care at all. The date-based proxy for delay is coarser than exact time-to-presentation measurements, and self-reported wound treatment is subject to recall bias. The clinic-opening period association is explicitly flagged as potentially confounded by factors the data cannot disentangle. Nevertheless, the large cohort size, careful data cleaning, and transparent statistical reporting give the findings credibility, and the consistency of the age effect with prior studies cited in the paper’s supplementary comparisons, including work from Hangzhou and Wuhan, strengthens the case that delayed presentation among older adults is a reproducible phenomenon rather than a local artifact. Funded by the Zhejiang Provincial Bureau of Disease Control and Prevention Science and Technology Program, the study received ethics approval from Tongde Hospital of Zhejiang Province and was conducted on de-identified electronic medical record data with a waiver of informed consent, in accordance with the 2013 Declaration of Helsinki. As rabies continues to kill an estimated tens of thousands of people worldwide each year, most of them in Asia and Africa, research of this kind translates a grim global statistic into actionable local insight. The message for clinicians and health communicators is clear: when it comes to rabies post-exposure prophylaxis, age is not just a demographic variable but a risk factor for the behaviors that allow a preventable disease to claim lives. Ensuring that older adults recognize exposures as emergencies, wash wounds immediately, and reach prophylaxis clinics without delay may prove one of the most cost-effective interventions available in the ongoing effort to eliminate rabies deaths. Subject of Research: Age-related delays in seeking rabies post-exposure prophylaxis and pre-hospital wound care after animal exposures Article Title: Age-related patterns in delayed presentation and pre-hospital wound management for rabies post-exposure prophylaxis: a retrospective hospital-based study in Hangzhou, China, 2022–2025 Article References: Yang, Z., Zhu, Y., Cao, K., Yi, X., Tian, S., Yan, B., & Zhao, Z. (2026). Age-related patterns in delayed presentation and pre-hospital wound management for rabies post-exposure prophylaxis: a retrospective hospital-based study in Hangzhou, China, 2022–2025. View five news elements
Time
The study was published on October 2, 2026, and the data analyzed spans from October 2022 to October 2025. The event date is not explicitly stated, but the findings are based on clinic presentations recorded during this period.
Location and population
The study was conducted in Hangzhou, China, focusing on a cohort of 3,327 patients who presented to the clinic after potential rabies exposures. The population of interest includes older adults aged 65 and older.
Event
A retrospective study from Hangzhou, China, published in BMC Infectious Diseases, found that older adults aged 65 and older are significantly more likely to delay seeking rabies treatment after animal bites compared to younger adults. This delay is measured by presenting on a subsequent calendar day rather than the same day as the injury. The study also examined pre-hospital wound management, finding that self-treatment was less common among older adults, though not statistically significant.
Scale, impact and risk
Of the 3,327 clinic presentations, 218 (6.6%) occurred on a subsequent calendar day after the injury. The adjusted odds ratio for older adults delaying presentation was 2.28, indicating they were more than twice as likely to delay. The study highlights the risk of preventable deaths due to delayed care, especially given the rabies virus's ability to travel along nerves and the potential for a one-day delay to impact outcomes.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was conducted using electronic medical records and applied rigorous exclusion criteria. The findings are based on a retrospective, single-center analysis, which has limitations in capturing all exposed individuals. The authors caution that the results for self-treatment among older adults are imprecise and require further confirmation. The study's design and statistical reporting provide credibility, but the data cannot determine whether delay and self-treatment behaviors are linked within the same individuals.

Bonnie Franklin 博士:狂犬病 仍然是一个真正的威胁,那么为什么圣巴巴拉县不需要 Cat 疫苗接种?

Dr. Bonnie Franklin: Rabies Is Still a Real Threat, So Why Doesn’t Santa Barbara County Require Cat Vaccinations?
新闻简报邦妮·富兰克林 (Bonnie Franklin) 博士对加利福尼亚州圣巴巴拉县 狂犬病 的持续威胁表示担忧,尽管对于非建制地区的猫缺乏强制性的 狂犬病 狂犬病。她强调,狂犬病 是一种致命病毒,影响包括人类在内的哺乳动物,并且不仅限于历史事件。在全球范围内,狂犬病 每年导致约 59,000 人死亡,而在美国,每年报告约 4,500 例动物病例。截至 2026 年 8 月中旬,加利福尼亚州已确诊 213 例 狂犬病 病例,其中 194 例涉及蝙蝠、18 例臭鼬和一只狗。圣巴巴拉县在 2026 年记录了四例确诊的蝙蝠 狂犬病 病例。富兰克林指出,狂犬病 可以通过咬伤或接触受感染的动物(例如患有狂犬病的蝙蝠或臭鼬)传播,并且猫比狗或牛更容易感染 狂犬病。她强调,如果在不寻常的地方发现蝙蝠,联系医疗保健专业人员的重要性,因为 狂犬病 只能在实验室中确认。文章还引用了新泽西州、马里兰州和北卡罗来纳州的 狂犬病 案例,其中一只患有狂犬病的海狸袭击了多人,而患有狂犬病的臭鼬在宠物动物园中暴露了山羊,导致数百人可能受到感染。富兰克林质疑为什么圣巴巴拉县不对非建制地区的猫要求 狂犬病 疫苗接种,并强调需要采取此类措施来保护宠物和人。
查看新闻五要素
时间
The article was published on 2026-09-30. Rabies cases in California were reported through mid-August 2026, and rabid bats have been found in 49 states as of the article's publication date.
地点与对象
本文重点介绍了加利福尼亚州圣巴巴拉县,并提到了非建制社区,例如 Hope Ranch、Isla Vista、Los Alamos、Los Olivos、Montecito、Orcutt、Santa Ynez 和 Summerland。它还在涉及海狸和山羊的 狂犬病 案件中引用了新泽西州、马里兰州和北卡罗来纳州。
事件
邦妮·富兰克林 (Bonnie Franklin) 讨论了 狂犬病 的持续威胁,强调 狂犬病 是一种致命的病毒性疾病,影响包括人类在内的哺乳动物,而且它不仅限于历史事件。她指出,狂犬病 在美国仍然流行,在蝙蝠、臭鼬和狗身上都有确诊病例,圣巴巴拉县和其他地区也有 狂犬病 病例。
规模、影响与风险
在全球范围内,狂犬病 每年导致约 59,000 人死亡。在美国,每年在动物中检测到约 4,500 例 狂犬病 病例,截至 2026 年 8 月中旬,加利福尼亚州有 213 例。在圣巴巴拉县,2026 年有 4 例蝙蝠确诊 狂犬病 病例。狂犬病 可以通过咬伤或接触受感染的动物进行传播,对于与山羊等可能被感染的动物接触的人来说,风险会更高。暴露于狂犬病的臭鼬之下。
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章讨论了圣巴巴拉县非建制地区的猫缺乏 狂犬病 疫苗接种 要求,尽管存在接触狂犬病蝙蝠和臭鼬的风险。它还强调了如果在不寻常的地方发现蝙蝠,联系医疗保健专业人员的重要性,因为 狂犬病 只能在实验室中确认。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dr. Bonnie Franklin: Rabies Is Still a Real Threat, So Why Doesn’t Santa Barbara County Require Cat Vaccinations?

News Brief
Dr. Bonnie Franklin raises concerns about the ongoing threat of rabies in Santa Barbara County, California, despite the lack of mandatory rabies vaccinations for cats in unincorporated areas. She highlights that rabies is a fatal virus affecting mammals, including humans, and is not limited to historical occurrences. Globally, rabies causes about 59,000 human deaths annually, while in the United States, around 4,500 animal cases are reported each year. In California, 213 rabies cases were confirmed through mid-August 2026, with 194 involving bats, 18 skunks, and one dog. Santa Barbara County has recorded four confirmed bat rabies cases in 2026. Franklin notes that rabies can be transmitted through bites or contact with infected animals, such as rabid bats or skunks, and that cats are more likely to contract rabies than dogs or cattle. She stresses the importance of contacting healthcare professionals if a bat is found in unusual locations, as rabies can only be confirmed in a laboratory. The article also references rabies cases in New Jersey, Maryland, and North Carolina, where a rabid beaver attacked multiple people and rabid skunks exposed goats at a petting zoo, leading to hundreds of potential human exposures. Franklin questions why Santa Barbara County does not require rabies vaccinations for cats in unincorporated areas, emphasizing the need for such measures to protect both pets and people.
Original source text (English)
Rabies is a fatal virus disease that attacks the central nervous system in mammals, including humans. It is not a disease that was only active historically. Worldwide, rabies causes about 59,000 human deaths annually. About 4,500 cases of rabies are detected in animals annually in the United States. This year in California, there have been 213 cases through mid-August. Of the confirmed rabies cases, 194 were bats,18 were skunks and one was a dog. In Santa Barbara County so far, there have been four confirmed cases of rabies in bats in 2026. In the United States we are finding rabies in mammals that were typically not observed to have rabies. In May, police in a suburban area of New Jersey responded to Lake Henry where an 8-year-old boy had been fishing from the shoreline when a beaver came out of the water and charged him. The boy tried to run away but tripped and fell. The beaver bit the boy, and the same beaver attacked multiple people at the lake. The beaver tested positive for rabies. There have also been confirmed cases of rabid beavers in Maryland and New York this year. In August, four goats from a traveling petting zoo tested positive following stops in Durham County, North Carolina. A rabid skunk had entered the goat’s enclosure and bit them. The goats then traveled to about 10 different stops across six North Carolina counties. Because they were baby goats, visitors often cuddled, pet and bottle fed them. Anyone who held or handled the goats may have been exposed to rabies. According to the North Carolina Department of Health and Human Services, exposed people are in the hundreds so far this year. California does not require cats to be vaccinated against rabies. Why? I am not sure. Cats are two times more likely to contract rabies than dogs or cattle. Your cat can get rabies from fighting with an aggressive rabid skunk or being bitten by or eating a rabid bat. When a bat gets rabies, it eventually falls to the ground and flails around with seizures. Cats see them as play toys. Cats may play with sick bats on the floor inside a house, as well as on the ground outside. You would never know if your outdoor cat encountered a rabid bat. The bite of a rabid bat leaves a very small puncture that typically is not apparent to owners nor is it painful to the cat. You cannot tell if a bat has rabies just by looking at it. Rabies can only be confirmed in a laboratory. Any bat that is active during the day or is found in a place where bats are not usually seen — like in your home or on your lawn — might be rabid. A bat that is unable to fly and is easily approached could be sick. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends that if you find a bat that is sick or dead inside or outside your house talk to a healthcare or public health professional and have the bat captured or picked up for possible rabies testing. According the CDC, bats are one of the most commonly reported rabid animals in the United States and are the leading cause of rabies deaths in people. Rabid bats have been found in 49 states, with only Hawai‘i being rabies-free. The good news is that most bats do not have rabies. A single bat can eat thousands of insects every night, including mosquitos and the moth stage of damaging caterpillars that strip avocado leaves. They also are nocturnal pollinators of our cherished avocados here in Santa Barbara County. Although rabies vaccinations are required for dogs, Santa Barbara County does not require cats to be vaccinated against rabies in its unincorporated communities. All eight incorporated cities — Buellton, Carpinteria, Goleta, Guadalupe, Lompoc, Santa Barbara, Santa Maria and Solvang — do require rabies vaccines for any cat over the age of 4 months old. Indoor or outdoor cats need vaccination, as there are indoor and outdoor bats in their environment. Unfortunately, the county does not require rabies vaccines for cats in unincorporated areas, including Hope Ranch, Isla Vista, Los Alamos, Los Olivos, Montecito, Orcutt, Santa Ynez and Summerland. Maybe someone should ask Santa Barbara County why. MORE BONNIE FRANKLIN DVM
View five news elements
Time
The article was published on 2026-09-30. Rabies cases in California were reported through mid-August 2026, and rabid bats have been found in 49 states as of the article's publication date.
Location and population
The article focuses on Santa Barbara County, California, and mentions unincorporated communities such as Hope Ranch, Isla Vista, Los Alamos, Los Olivos, Montecito, Orcutt, Santa Ynez, and Summerland. It also references New Jersey, Maryland, and North Carolina in the context of rabies cases involving beavers and goats.
Event
Bonnie Franklin discusses the ongoing threat of rabies, highlighting that rabies is a fatal virus disease affecting mammals, including humans, and that it is not limited to historical occurrences. She notes that rabies is still prevalent in the United States, with confirmed cases in bats, skunks, and dogs, and that there are rabies cases in Santa Barbara County and other regions.
Scale, impact and risk
Worldwide, rabies causes about 59,000 human deaths annually. In the United States, approximately 4,500 cases of rabies are detected in animals annually, with 213 cases in California through mid-August 2026. In Santa Barbara County, there were four confirmed rabies cases in bats in 2026. Rabies can be transmitted through bites or contact with infected animals, and the risk is heightened for people who interact with animals like goats that may have been exposed to rabid skunks.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article discusses the lack of rabies vaccination requirements for cats in unincorporated areas of Santa Barbara County, despite the risk of exposure to rabid bats and skunks. It also emphasizes the importance of contacting healthcare professionals if a bat is found in unusual locations, as rabies can only be confirmed in a laboratory.

密苏里州 狂犬病 风险敞口增加

An increase in rabies exposure spreads across Missouri
新闻简报据报道,密苏里州各地 狂犬病 暴露量有所增加,促使疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 发布官方健康警报。从 2026 年 7 月到 8 月,CDC 收到的 狂犬病 询问比 2025 年同期增加了 17%。该州在 2026 年记录了 36 例确诊的动物 狂犬病 病例,其中蝙蝠和臭鼬是主要传播者。一旦出现症状,狂犬病 几乎 100% 致命,但如果在症状出现之前服用,则可以使用 狂犬病 暴露后预防 (rPEP) 来预防。rPEP 包括在第 0 天、第 3 天、第 7 天和第 14 天进行四次注射。动物控制专家 Kevin Meyers 强调,狂犬病 测试对于确认动物体内是否存在病毒至关重要。他还建议宠物主人确保他们的宠物接种疫苗,并建议避免与野生动物接触。如果被咬伤,个人应对动物进行 10 天的监测;如果保持健康,则不存在 狂犬病 传播 的风险。治疗 的费用很高,通常每人 11,000 美元到 14,000 美元不等。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据疾病预防控制中心网站称,由于人类接触患有狂犬病或可能患狂犬病的动物的报告增加,美国疾病控制与预防中心发布了官方健康警报。据该中心网站显示,从 2026 年 7 月到 8 月,该中心收到的 狂犬病 询问比 2025 年同期增加了 17%。据报道,密苏里州各地 狂犬病 暴露量有所增加,2026 年确诊了 36 例动物 狂犬病 病例。根据密苏里州卫生部网站,该州的主要传播者是蝙蝠和臭鼬。
地点与对象
接触这种疾病的动物包括已知的 狂犬病 媒介 以及不太常见的动物。
事件
据报道,密苏里州各地 狂犬病 暴露量有所增加,疾病控制与预防中心发布了官方健康警报。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年,密苏里州已确诊 36 例动物 狂犬病 病例。据 CDC 称,通常情况下,一旦出现症状,狂犬病 几乎 100% 致命。然而,如果在患者症状出现之前给予 狂犬病 暴露后预防 (rPEP),则这种情况是可以预防的。
应对、状态与不确定性
哥伦比亚/布恩县公共卫生和公众服务部的动物控制专家 Kevin Meyers 表示,rPEP 是一系列四次注射,分别在第 0 天、第 3 天、第 7 天和第 14 天进行注射,只要在症状出现之前注射,成功率就是 100%。如果发现需要重新安置的动物,请联系动物控制中心,以便他们尽快将其移走。如果您被动物咬伤,请在咬伤后 10 天内密切关注该动物。如果 10 天后动物仍然活着且健康,则不存在传播病毒的风险。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

An increase in rabies exposure spreads across Missouri

News Brief
An increase in rabies exposure has been reported across Missouri, prompting the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to issue an official health alert. From July through August 2026, the CDC received 17% more rabies inquiries compared to the same period in 2025. The state has recorded 36 confirmed animal rabies cases in 2026, with bats and skunks being the primary transmitters. Rabies is nearly 100% fatal once symptoms begin, but it is preventable with rabies post-exposure prophylaxis (rPEP) if administered before symptoms appear. rPEP involves four shots given on days zero, three, seven, and 14. Kevin Meyers, an animal control specialist, emphasized that rabies testing is essential to confirm the presence of the virus in an animal. He also advised pet owners to ensure their pets are vaccinated and recommended avoiding contact with wildlife. If bitten, individuals should monitor the animal for 10 days; if it remains healthy, there is no risk of rabies transmission. The cost of treatment is high, typically ranging from $11,000 to $14,000 per person.
Original source text (English)

Reports and Public Files Contact Us Tax ID number Missouri News An increase in rabies exposure spreads across Missouri KBIA | By KJ Drazen, Columbia Missourian Published October 2, 2026 at 8:08 AM CDT Facebook Bluesky Twitter LinkedIn Email The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention issued an official health alert due to an increase of reports in human exposure to rabid or possibly rabid animals, according to the CDC’s website. The CDC has received 17% more rabies inquiries from July through August 2026 than it did during the same time period in 2025, according to the center’s website. Exposures to this disease have been coming from known rabies vectors as well as animals that it is less commonly seen in. Normally, rabies is nearly 100% fatal once symptoms begin, according to the CDC. However, it is preventable if rabies post-exposure prophylaxis (rPEP) is administered to the patient before their symptoms start. Kevin Meyers, the animal control specialist for Columbia/Boone County Department of Public Health and Human Services, said rPEP is a series of four shots, administered on days zero, three, seven and 14, with a success rate of 100% as long as it is given prior to symptoms starting. To date, there have been 36 confirmed animal rabies cases across Missouri in 2026. The main transmitters in the state have been bats and skunks, according to the Missouri Department of Health’s website. But these treatments — while necessary for survival — do not come without a cost. “The cost of treatment is incredibly high, usually ranging between $11,000 to $14,000 per person,” Meyers said. Meyers also provided information people should know to help keep these exposure numbers low. First of all, he said, make sure to have all pets vaccinated by a licensed veterinarian and keep them up to date on immunizations as they need renewal. If you are bitten by an animal, keep an eye on the animal for 10 days after the bite occurs, Meyers said. If the animal is still alive and healthy after those 10 days, there is no risk that it can spread the virus. Finally, do not touch wildlife. If an animal is found that needs to be relocated, contact Animal Control so they can remove it as soon as possible, Meyers said. If you have been bitten or believe you have been exposed, Animal Control can send a sample for rabies testing. “Sending off a sample is the only way to know with 100% certainty whether or not the animal was carrying the rabies virus,” Meyers said. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

“狂犬病 风险是真实存在的”:BC省潜在的病毒性疾病风险激增 - KelownaNow

'Rabies risk is real': Potential exposures to viral disease surging across BC - KelownaNow
新闻简报据不列颠哥伦比亚省疾病控制中心 (BCCDC) 称,不列颠哥伦比亚省报告称,2026 年人类潜在 狂犬病 暴露病例将显着增加,从 2025 年的 931 例增至 1,598 例。正如 BCCDC 声明中指出的那样,这种上升可能归因于与受感染动物的更多实际接触或公众意识的提高。BCCDC 强调,蝙蝠中的 狂犬病 病例数量并未增加,表明感染风险并未增加。一旦出现 狂犬病 的症状,这种疾病几乎总是致命的,但早期的医疗干预可以预防它。BCCDC 建议蝙蝠研究人员和兽医等高危人群进行预暴露 狂犬病,但没有省级强制要求 狂犬病 疫苗。BC省的宠物主人也发现他们对给宠物接种疫苗的兴趣越来越大,一些诊所报告询问量增加了20%。然而,由于错误信息,特别是来自饲养员的错误信息,疫苗 的犹豫仍然存在,许多宠物主人仍然对 狂犬病 疫苗 的必要性感到矛盾。BCCDC 继续调查接触量激增的原因,并建议在可能接触蝙蝠后立即采取行动,包括清洗伤口和寻求医疗护理。
查看新闻五要素
时间
BCCDC 报告称,2026 年人类 狂犬病 潜在暴露量激增,达到 1,598 例,而 2025 年为 931 例。潜在暴露量的增加尚未完全了解,因为这可能是由于公众意识增强或实际暴露率增加所致。接触后可能需要三到八周才会出现症状 (N20)。
地点与对象
该报告重点关注不列颠哥伦比亚省(BC),特别提到考特尼和坎贝尔河是蝙蝠被猫带回家的地方。受影响人群包括人类和宠物,尤其是猫和狗,他们可能通过与蝙蝠接触而接触到狂犬病。BC CDC 还指出,蝙蝠是 BC 省唯一携带该疾病且未被其杀死的野生动物 (N16)。
事件
据 BCCDC 称,2026 年,不列颠哥伦比亚省的潜在人类 狂犬病 暴露量激增。这种增加可能是由于更多的实际曝光或公众意识的提高。BCCDC还提到,蝙蝠中的狂犬病病例数量似乎没有增加,没有理由相信BC省(N12)感染狂犬病的风险增加。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年,有 1,598 人可能接触 狂犬病,而 2025 年为 931 人。一旦出现症状,这种疾病几乎总是致命的 (N6)。然而,早期医疗干预可以预防疾病的发展(N22)。BCCDC 还指出,在草原地区,臭鼬可以携带 狂犬病,而在加拿大东部,浣熊和狐狸是携带者(N18、N19)。
应对、状态与不确定性
BCCDC 正在调查潜在暴露的增加,但尚未确定确切原因。他们认为这可能是由于公众意识的提高或实际曝光的增加。BCCDC 建议蝙蝠研究人员、兽医和其他面临风险的人接受 狂犬病 预暴露 疫苗 (N26)。然而,BC 省 (N27) 没有省级 狂犬病 疫苗 强制令。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

'Rabies risk is real': Potential exposures to viral disease surging across BC - KelownaNow

News Brief
British Columbia has reported a significant increase in potential human rabies exposures in 2026, with 1,598 cases compared to 931 in 2025, according to the BC Centre for Disease Control (BCCDC). This rise may be attributed to either more actual contact with infected animals or heightened public awareness, as noted in the BCCDC's statement. The BCCDC emphasizes that the number of rabies cases in bats has not increased, suggesting no heightened risk of infection. Once symptoms of rabies appear, the disease is almost always fatal, but early medical intervention can prevent it. The BCCDC recommends pre-exposure vaccination for those at risk, such as bat researchers and veterinarians, but there are no provincial mandates for rabies vaccines. Pet owners in BC are also seeing increased interest in vaccinating their pets, with some clinics reporting a 20% rise in inquiries. However, vaccine hesitancy persists due to misinformation, particularly from breeders, and many pet owners remain conflicted about the necessity of rabies vaccines. The BCCDC continues to investigate the cause of the exposure spike and advises immediate action following potential contact with bats, including washing wounds and seeking medical care.
Original source text (English)
British Columbia has seen a spike in potential human rabies exposures in 2026, with 1,598 potential exposures compared with 931 potential exposures in 2025, according to the BC Centre for Disease Control, or BCCDC. A potential human rabies exposure means there’s a risk that a person has been exposed to rabies, so they receive treatment to prevent the disease from developing. This number may capture people who were exposed to animals who did not carry the disease but were given treatment just to be safe. A person is considered to have rabies once they start showing symptoms. Once this happens there is no treatment. The disease is nearly always fatal, with a few very rare exceptions. Over the last 104 years there have been only 28 human rabies cases in Canada, the BCCDC said. Two of them were in B.C. Rabies deaths are much higher in countries with higher incidences of dog bites, in particular in poorer, more rural communities that don’t have adequate access to post-exposure treatment. The United States is also reporting an increase in potential human rabies exposures this year, but not an increase in rabies cases. Photo credit: Cory Olson/WCS Canada It’s not clear if the increase in potential exposures is because more people are coming into contact with potentially infected animals or if there’s more public awareness about the importance of seeking medical attention after potential exposure, the BCCDC said in an emailed statement. The number of rabies cases in bats does not appear to be increasing, so “there is no reason to believe there is an increased risk of rabies infection” in B.C. or elsewhere in Canada, the BCCDC said. In B.C. any physical contact with a bat is considered an exposure because one in 200 bats can carry rabies. If the bat is captured, is tested and is negative, it is no longer considered an exposure. Bats are the only wild animal in B.C. that carries the disease and is not killed by it. In the Prairies, skunks can carry rabies. In Eastern Canada, racoons and foxes also carry the disease, and in parts of the United States opossums can live with the disease, Heather James, a small-animal veterinarian in Prince George and a board member of the Society of BC Veterinarians, told The Tyee. Symptoms can take three to eight weeks to develop after exposure. Once symptoms start, the disease is always fatal in humans and pets, James said. But medical intervention can prevent the disease from developing. After a potential exposure, the wound or area of contact should immediately be washed with soap and water for at least 15 minutes, according to HealthLink BC. Next, a person should seek immediate care from a health-care provider, who will assess and decide if the person needs rabies immune globulin and/or the rabies vaccine. If administered early, these measures almost always prevent a person from developing rabies. The BCCDC recommends that bat researchers, veterinarians and other people who may come in contact with the virus get the rabies pre-exposure vaccine. In B.C. there are no provincial rabies vaccine mandates, the BCCDC said. Some employers may, however, require vaccines as part of occupational health and safety policies. In Ontario and parts of the United States, rabies vaccines are mandatory for pets, James said. In B.C., many veterinary hospitals, boarding facilities and groomers will also require pets to be vaccinated to protect staff, she added. Public awareness about rabies is high right now because of a CBC article published this summer about an 11-year-old boy in Ontario who died from rabies in 2024, the BCCDC said. There are no other trends in bat and animal populations that would help explain the increase in potential rabies exposures, it added. Rabies vaccines for pets The American Animal Hospital Association recommends that all pets, regardless of whether they are indoor or outdoor pets, be vaccinated against rabies, James said. This protects pets if a bat ever gets in the home, or if a cat or dog comes home with a bat in its mouth. When a pet comes home with a bat, it’s recommended to take the bat to a veterinarian so it can be tested for rabies. Over the summer this process identified two bats, brought home by cats, that had rabies in Courtenay and Campbell River, said Leanne Kelly, a registered veterinary technologist at Van Isle Veterinary Hospital. The cats were given post-exposure treatment and did not develop rabies, but the cases have created a spike in pet owners wanting to vaccinate their pets against the disease, Kelly said. Many pet owners will vaccinate their pets but skip the rabies vaccine because they don’t think it’s necessary, she said. She estimates that over the summer her clinic saw a 20 per cent increase in pet owners asking for their pets to be vaccinated and a 20 per cent increase in pet owners calling to ask if the rabies vaccine is available. “I think it’s going to continue to go up as people get more and more aware that the rabies risk is real,” she said. While people occasionally post misinformation about pet vaccines on social media, veterinarians and vet techs in Prince George, Courtenay and Kelowna told The Tyee they rarely meet pet owners who are outright hostile to them or refuse all vaccines for their pets. Most people are just “conflicted” and overwhelmed by all of the information, and misinformation, on the internet, James said. Pet owners will say they’re worried the antigen in the vaccine can cause an allergic reaction in their pet, she said. But that’s misrepresenting the situation a little. An antigen is any substance that can be recognized by an immune system. Antigens are in everything, including cheeseburgers, vaccines and toothpaste. An immune system decides to react or not react every time it is exposed to an antigen, and it is exposed to thousands of antigens every day. There’s only one antigen in a rabies vaccine for the body to potentially react to, which is a fraction of the number of antigens in a cheeseburger, James added. James and Kelly said breeders seem to be a source of pet vaccine misinformation, telling new pet owners what vaccines they should and shouldn’t give their pets. However, pet owners are generally open to talk with veterinarians about their concerns, and the majority of them fully vaccinate their pets. Marco Veenis, a veterinarian at Okanagan Veterinary Hospital who has been practising since 1988, said he’s seen vaccine hesitancy growing over the last decade. He said this is likely due to the rise of social media and online misinformation. Patients will say they don’t believe in vaccines, but when you ask them why, they don’t have an answer, he said. Vets will recommend vaccines based on a pet’s lifestyle, for example if they go to a dog park or travel internationally, Veenis said, but ultimately it’s up to the pet owner to decide what shots their pet gets. “It’s not my job to tell them what to do. It’s my job to make sure they can make a properly informed decision,” he added. James said it’s recommended that puppies and kittens be vaccinated for rabies when they are as young as three months old, again at one year and then every three years. The vaccine costs about $30. With a vet fee and other charges, for example the cost of other vaccines, a visit may cost between $125 and $200. James has seen rabies in cats and dogs, and in horses and cattle when she worked in Saskatchewan. “Sometimes they can’t swallow or walk,” she said. “It’s just really scary.” If an unvaccinated pet is potentially exposed to rabies, a vet will follow BCCDC guidelines to determine if the pet can be given a post-exposure vaccine, if they need to quarantine the pet or if they must euthanize it. Pet owners and people who came in contact with the pet will also have to be assessed by a health-care provider. The province’s rabies protocols are ultimately designed to keep humans safe. “We don’t want to lose our cats or dogs,” she said, “but at the end of the day it’s a different kind of tragic when a human dies of rabies.” Another vaccine-preventable illness that’s becoming more common is leptospirosis, a bacterial disease that causes kidney and liver failure. Leptospirosis tends to have more serious consequences for dogs than it has for cats. The disease is not as common in cats. Leptospirosis is transmitted through wildlife or rat urine. The American Animal Hospital Association recently declared the vaccine for leptospirosis to be a “core” vaccine for dogs because of its increasing prevalence, James said. No leptospirosis vaccine is currently available for cats. Outdoor cats are more likely to contract leptospirosis than indoor cats, according to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Leptospirosis can transfer to humans, where it can also cause kidney and liver failure. If you appreciate what we do, we ask that you consider supporting our local independent news platform. British Columbia has experienced a surge in potential human rabies exposures in 2026, according to the BCCDC. This increase may be due to either more actual exposure or heightened public awareness. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabies: This is what every pet owner should know - The Citizen

News Brief
The article, published on 2026-09-29 by The Citizen, discusses rabies in South Africa, highlighting that the virus occurs in all nine provinces, with dog rabies cycles concentrated in the eastern regions. It explains that rabies can be transmitted through bites or contact with infected saliva, and that any mammal, including pets, wildlife, livestock, and humans, is at risk. The article notes that once clinical signs appear, the disease is typically terminal, and immediate medical attention is crucial. It also mentions that unvaccinated or inadequately vaccinated animals can spread the virus, even if they appear healthy. A map of reported outbreaks between 2014 and 2015 is referenced, and the importance of community vaccination is stressed as a preventive measure. The information is based on available data and is presented as general guidance rather than confirmed outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
Animal rabies occurs in all nine provinces, with dog rabies cycles mainly concentrated in the eastern parts of the country (Limpopo, Mpumalanga, KwaZulu-Natal, Eastern Cape provinces and the eastern Free State border with Lesotho). Dogs and cats are often moved between provinces by travellers, workers, or holiday-goers, which can easily cause a spillover of rabies to any area if these animals are unvaccinated or inadequately vaccinated and incubating the disease. Remember that any mammal can become infected with rabies, this includes pets, wildlife, livestock and humans. Wildlife species that can host rabies include jackals, foxes, mongooses, aardwolf and Cape fur seals. A map showing reported Rabies outbreaks in South Africa between January 2014 and December 2015. Image: SA Goernment News. If your dogs and cats are vaccinated against rabies, they form a protective barrier for your family and protect you and your beloved pets. The same is true if everyone in your community ensures that their dogs and cats are vaccinated. What is Rabies? Rabies is a fatal disease caused by a virus that can affect all mammals, including humans. The virus is present in high concentrations in the saliva of infected animals and is transmitted by bite wounds. The virus replicates in the tissues, subsequently entering the nerve endings in and around the wound. It can travel along these nerves and gain entry into the central nervous system, rapidly spreading to the brain stem and the forebrain. Typical clinical signs in rabid animals or humans occur once the rabies virus has reached the brain tissue. Once these clinical signs are noted, the disease is usually at a terminal stage and medical intervention is seldom successful. The rabies virus can spread to the salivary glands before the infected animal starts to exhibit symptoms of clinical rabies; the disease can thus be transmitted by infected animals that appear outwardly normal. Image supplied by SA Government News Signs of rabies in dogs and cats: Although signs may vary, infected animals may initially show changes in behaviour, like nervousness and anxiety. These symptoms can last for two or three days. They progress to irritability, photophobia (fear of light), biting, snapping, incoordination and seizures. Some animals may show signs of weakness, incoordination and paralysis, which also affects the muscles used for swallowing. How can you, your dog or cat contract Rabies? Rabies is spread primarily through infected saliva. The virus needs to gain entry into the body, thus normally through an injury to the skin. This usually occurs during a bite wound from a rabid animal. Transmission may also occur if infected saliva comes into contact with open wounds, for example, by licking. What to do if you get bitten by a rabid animal Should you be bitten by a suspected rabid animal, the first step is to wash the wound thoroughly with running water and a disinfectant. You will need to see a doctor immediately so that they can begin post- exposure treatment. (Source: Bayer (Pty) Ltd., Animal Health Division www.bayeranimalhealth.co.za). At Caxton, every story is written by humans. We use AI only to perform quality checks - never to generate the news. Happy reading!

自由县在狐狸袭击后发布 狂犬病 警报 - WTOC

Rabies alert issued in Liberty County following fox attack - WTOC
新闻简报2026 年 9 月 28 日星期一,佐治亚州自由县发布了 狂犬病 警报,当地居民遭到狐狸袭击。2026 年 9 月 29 日星期二,这只狐狸在州公共卫生实验室检测出 狂犬病 呈阳性。受害者被咬或抓伤,目前正在接受暴露后医疗护理。卫生官员警告说,狂犬病 是佐治亚州沿海地区的地方病,通过狐狸、浣熊和蝙蝠等受感染动物的咬伤或抓伤传播。一旦出现症状,该病毒几乎总是致命的。自由县卫生部门建议居民避免接触不熟悉的动物,给宠物接种疫苗,不要处理或收养野生动物。该事件凸显了立即就医并向地方当局报告动物遭遇的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件发生在2026年9月28日星期一,狂犬病警报于9月星期三发布。受害者目前正在接受暴露后医疗护理。
地点与对象
佐治亚州自由县,特别是海恩斯维尔的布莱恩特公共公园附近。受影响的人群包括自由县的居民和被狐狸袭击的受害者。
事件
一只狐狸袭击了自由县的一名当地居民,该动物的 狂犬病 检测呈阳性。利伯蒂县卫生部门发布了 狂犬病 警报,敦促居民采取预防措施。
规模、影响与风险
一名居民遭到一只狂犬病狐狸的袭击。狂犬病 是佐治亚州沿海地区的特有种,在浣熊、狐狸和蝙蝠等野生动物中传播。一旦出现症状,这种病毒几乎总是致命的,通过受感染动物的咬伤或抓伤传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
这只狐狸被杀死并运往州公共卫生实验室进行检测。卫生官员已向居民发布了预防提示,包括避免接触不熟悉的动物、保持宠物疫苗接种的最新状态以及不要处理或收养野生动物。受害者已收到通知并正在接受医疗护理。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Rabies alert issued in Liberty County following fox attack - WTOC

News Brief
A rabies alert was issued in Liberty County, Georgia, following a fox attack on a local resident on Monday, Sept. 28, 2026. The fox tested positive for rabies at a state public health laboratory on Tuesday, Sept. 29, 2026. The victim, who was bitten or scratched, is currently receiving post-exposure medical care. Health officials warn that rabies is endemic to coastal Georgia and spreads through bites or scratches from infected animals like foxes, raccoons, and bats. The virus is nearly always fatal once symptoms appear. The Liberty County Health Department has advised residents to avoid unfamiliar animals, keep pets vaccinated, and not handle or adopt wildlife. The incident highlights the importance of immediate medical attention and reporting animal encounters to local authorities.
Original source text (English)

Sports Scoreboard High School Sports Atlanta Braves Savannah Bananas Savannah State Georgia Southern End Zone Golf Community Birthday Club Calendar Live Healthy Morning Break Pet Pics Top Teacher The Big Give Back Senior Salutes WTOC Toy Drive Morning Break Things to Do Food Arts and Entertainment Health Music Style DIY Sponsored About Us Contact Us Download Our Apps Internships Meet The Team Antenna Tips Advertising Programming InvestigateTV PowerNation Aging Untold Local News Live NextGen TV Contests Digital Marketing Gray AI Policy Rabies alert issued in Liberty County following fox attack By Christa Ross Published: Sep. 29, 2026 at 9:21 PM UTC Share Add Us On GoogleAdd as a preferred source on Google HINESVILLE, Ga. (WTOC) - The Liberty County Health Department is urging residents to take precautions after a fox that attacked a local resident tested positive for rabies. The incident happened Monday, Sept. 28, outside a home near Bryant Commons Park in Hinesville. According to health officials, the resident reported being attacked by the animal. The fox was killed and transported to a state public health laboratory, where it tested positive for the deadly virus on Tuesday, Sept. 29. The victim has been notified and is currently receiving post-exposure medical care. Health officials noted that rabies is endemic to coastal Georgia and circulates primarily among wild animals such as raccoons, foxes, and bats. The virus is nearly always fatal once symptoms appear, spreading through bites or scratches from infected animals. Prevention Tips for Residents The Liberty County Health Department’s Environmental Health Office recommends the following safety measures: Avoid unfamiliar animals: Steer clear of wild, stray, or unknown animals, even if they appear friendly. Keep pet vaccinations current: Dogs and cats should receive their initial rabies vaccine after 12 weeks of age, followed by a booster within one year and routine shots every one to three years. Eliminate food sources: Secure outdoor garbage cans and do not leave pet food outside overnight. Do not handle or adopt wildlife: Never bring wild animals into your home or attempt to nurse sick wildlife back to health. Contact local animal control or licensed wildlife rescuers instead. Educate children: Remind kids to “love your own, leave other animals alone.” Signs of rabies in wildlife include uncharacteristic aggression, an unusual lack of fear around humans, sudden behavior changes, paralysis, or foaming at the mouth. Anyone bitten or scratched by an animal should seek immediate medical attention and report the encounter to local animal control and county environmental health officials. Copyright 2026 WTOC. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health warns of an increase in bat attacks that can transmit rabies - Diari ARA

News Brief
Health authorities in Catalonia, Spain, have issued a warning about an increase in bat attacks that can transmit rabies. According to the data, there were 86 bat attacks recorded between January 1st and August 12th, 2026, which is 16 more than the total number in 2025. This rise has prompted the Department of Health to advise the public to avoid contact with bats. The health data also indicate that bat attacks are more frequent during the summer months, with almost half of the bites occurring in June, July, and mid-August. In response, 67% of the exposures reported to the XVEC required rabies immunoglobulin and vaccination follow-up. These actions are taken as a preventive measure, as bats are currently the main carriers of the rabies virus in the region. The situation is under investigation, and the health authorities are emphasizing the need for public awareness and caution.

Getty ARA 29/09/2026 - 12:59 h Unverified translation CAT CAST Add us on Google BarcelonaCatalonia has been free of endemic terrestrial rabies for some time, but in recent years there has been growing concern about the airborne transmission of this disease. To the point that the Department of Health has issued a call to the population to avoid contact with bats. It does so as a preventive measure because attacks by these animals—which are currently the main carriers of this disease—are on the rise. During 2026, there have been 23% more attacks in Catalonia by bats compared to those that occurred throughout the previous year. Of the 198 attacks recorded between January 1st and August 12th in the Principality, 86 were carried out by these winged mammals, 16 more than were detected during the entirety of 2025. The rest of the attacks are from dogs. In this case, during the first eight months of the year, one more (56) has been recorded than during all of 2025. Health data show that bat attacks are more frequent during the summer. In the months of June, July, and mid-August alone, almost half of the bites have occurred, 45%. In Catalonia, as in other communities and European countries, the sporadic presence of bats infected with the European rabies virus has been confirmed. A deadly disease Rabies is a very serious disease, almost always fatal, that can affect all mammals. The virus is usually transmitted through the saliva of infected animals through bites, scratches, or licks on open wounds or mucous membranes. In order to prevent infection in people who have suffered an attack by animals considered at risk, the administration of rabies immunoglobulin and the follow-up of a vaccination schedule is necessary, subsequent to the risk exposure. This year, 67% of the exposures reported to the XVEC have required these actions in light of the possibility of contagion. Home Games Sections

蝙蝠触碰他的脸,但没有受伤...11岁男孩死亡 - 헬스조선

News article (Korean original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报2024年夏天,加拿大安大略省一名11岁男孩在睡觉时脸部被蝙蝠触碰后死于狂犬病。他的家人最初没有就医,因为没有明显的咬伤或抓伤痕迹,而且蝙蝠也没有表现出异常行为。然而,19天后,他开始出现麻木、面部肿胀和神智不清等症状,因此前往医院就诊。住院第四天,经检测确诊为狂犬病。尽管在重症监护室接受了治疗,但他还是在住院第17天去世了。加拿大医学会杂志 (CMAJ) 报道的这一案例强调了 暴露后预防 对于 暴露后预防 的重要性,即使没有明显的伤害。狂犬病是一种致命性疾病,一旦出现症状就没有特效,主要通过受感染动物的唾液传播。该事件凸显了 狂犬病 传播 通过粘膜或开放性伤口传播的风险,特别是在蝙蝠等野生动物常见的地区。这是自 1967 年以来安大略省首例本地感染的 狂犬病 病例,它提醒人们在潜在接触后需要保持警惕并立即采取医疗行动。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

News article (Korean original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Korean)
A Bat Touched His Face, but There Wasn't a Wound... 11-Year-Old Boy Dies - 헬스조선
News Brief
An 11-year-old boy in Ontario, Canada, died of rabies after a bat touched his face while he was sleeping in the summer of 2024. His family did not seek medical attention initially because there were no visible bite or scratch marks, and the bat did not show unusual behavior. However, 19 days later, he began experiencing symptoms such as numbness, facial swelling, and confusion, which led to a hospital visit. On the fourth day of hospitalization, he was diagnosed with rabies through testing. Despite receiving treatment in the intensive care unit, he passed away on the 17th day of hospitalization. This case, reported by the Canadian Medical Association Journal (CMAJ), underscores the importance of post-exposure prophylaxis for rabies, even in the absence of visible injuries. Rabies is a fatal disease with no effective treatment once symptoms appear, and it is primarily transmitted through the saliva of infected animals. The incident highlights the risk of rabies transmission via mucous membranes or open wounds, especially in areas where wild animals like bats are common. This is the first locally acquired rabies case in Ontario since 1967, and it serves as a reminder of the need for vigilance and prompt medical action following potential exposure.
Original source text (Korean)
News Issues Wellness Beauty Pharma KOREAN SEARCH Search Health Chosun A Bat Touched His Face, but There Wasn't a Wound... 11-Year-Old Boy Dies By Choi Soo Yeon Updated 2026/07/03 00:30 A case has been reported of an 11-year-old boy who died of rabies after a bat touched his face while he was sleeping./Photo=Getty Images Bank A case has been reported of an 11-year-old boy who died of rabies after a bat touched his face while he was sleeping. On the 30th, local time, the Canadian Medical Association Journal, CMAJ, presented the case to help prevent similar incidents in the future. In the summer of 2024, an 11-year-old boy sleeping at a cottage in Ontario, Canada, found a bat sitting on his nose. Startled, the boy swatted the bat away with his hand. Because there were no visible bite or scratch marks and the bat did not display unusual behavior, his family did not seek medical attention. However, 19 days after coming into contact with the bat, the boy began experiencing numbness and reduced sensation on one side of his face. His face then became swollen and his appetite declined, followed by difficulty swallowing, vomiting, fever, confusion and hallucinations. He was initially mistaken as having a herpesvirus infection and sent home, but when his symptoms worsened the next day, he returned to the hospital. He was ultimately diagnosed with rabies through testing on the fourth day of hospitalization. The boy received treatment in the intensive care unit but died on the 17th day of hospitalization. Rabies can be transmitted when saliva from an infected animal comes into contact not only with a bite wound but also with the mucous membranes of the nose, mouth or eyes, or with an open wound. In this case, too, there were no visible bite marks, but he was presumed to have been infected through this route. ◇ Extremely high fatality rate after symptoms appear Rabies is a fatal infectious disease for which there is virtually no treatment once symptoms appear. It is primarily a zoonotic disease that occurs when the saliva of an animal carrying the rabies virus enters the body through a bite wound, abrasion or mucous membrane. About 99% of human rabies cases are transmitted by dogs, but according to the World Health Organization, in North and South America, where rabies is largely controlled, wild animals such as bats are considered the primary sources of infection. This is the first locally acquired case of rabies in Ontario, Canada, since 1967. Rabies is rare in Canada, with only 28 human cases reported since 1924, but the WHO has reported that tens of thousands of people die from rabies worldwide every year, with children under 15 accounting for about 40% of the victims. No confirmed human rabies cases have been reported in South Korea since 2004. However, rabies virus continues to be detected, particularly in wild raccoons, in areas bordering North Korea, including Incheon, Gyeonggi and Gangwon, prompting disease control authorities to urge people not to let their guard down. If you are bitten by a wild animal or dog suspected of having rabies, or come into direct contact with a bat, you should wash the wound with soap and water for at least 15 minutes and then seek medical attention, even if the wound is small. Most cases of rabies can be prevented by administering post-exposure prophylaxis, PEP, after exposure and before symptoms appear, but it is difficult to expect preventive effects once symptoms have begun. Dr. Brian Hummel, a pediatric infectious disease specialist at McMaster Children's Hospital in Canada, said, “Even if there are no visible bite or scratch marks, post-exposure prophylaxis is necessary if you have had direct contact with a bat,” adding, “This should be kept firmly in mind, especially during the summer, when contact between people and bats increases.” This article has been translated AI.
